Unveiling Correlated Two-dimensional Topological Insulators through Fermionic Tensor Network States – Classification, Edge Theories and Variational Wavefunctions
Chao Xu, Yixin Ma, Shenghan Jiang

Abstract
The study of topological band insulators has revealed fascinating phases characterized by band topology indices and anomalous boundary modes protected by global symmetries. In strongly correlated systems, where the traditional notion of electronic bands becomes obsolete, it has been established that the topological insulator phases persist as stable phases, separate from the trivial insulators. However, due to the inability to express the ground states of such systems as Slater determinants, the formulation of generic variational wavefunctions for numerical simulations is highly desirable. In this paper, we tackle this challenge for two-dimensional topological insulators by developing a comprehensive framework for fermionic tensor network states. Starting from simple assumptions, we obtain possible sets of tensor equations for any given symmetry group, capturing consistent relations governing symmetry transformation rules on tensor legs. We then examine the connections between these tensor equations and non-chiral topological insulators by construing edge theories and extracting quantum anomaly data from each set of tensor equations. By exhaustively exploring all possible sets of equations, we achieve a systematic classification of non-chiral topological insulator phases. Imposing the solutions of a given set of equations onto local tensors, we obtain generic variational wavefunctions for corresponding topological insulator phases. Our methodology provides an important step towards simulating topological insulators in strongly correlated systems. We discuss the limitations and potential generalizations of our results, paving the way for further advancements in this field.



I
Introduction

Since the groundbreaking discovery of topological insulators
Kane and Mele 2005a
;
Konig et al. 2007
;
Fu et al. 2007
;
Hasan and Kane 2010
;
Qi and Zhang 2011
, the interplay between topology and symmetry has emerged as an exciting research frontier in both non-interacting and interacting systems

1
1

1

When first proposed, topological insulators means 2D or 3D band insulators with helical Dirac edge mode protected by charge conservation and time reversal symmetry.
In this work, we use a broader definition, referring to electronic insulators with anomalous edge modes protected by any symmetry group.
.
In weakly interacting systems, topological insulators are classified based on their band topology, with symmetry indicators providing valuable insights
Schnyder et al. 2008
;
Kitaev 2009
;
Chiu et al. 2016
;
Kane and Mele 2005b
;
Fu and Kane 2007
;
Kruthoff et al. 2017
;
Po et al. 2017
;
Song et al. 2018
;
Tang et al. 2019
;
Po 2020
.
The generalization of topological insulators, along with topological superconductors, to interacting systems has given rise to the fascinating realm of fermionic symmetry-protected topological (FSPT) phases
Gu and Wen 2014
;
Wang and Senthil 2014
;
Kapustin et al. 2015
;
Wang and Gu 2018
;
Wang and Gu 2020
;
Aasen et al. 2021
;
Barkeshli et al. 2022
.

In contrast to the fractional topological insulators
Levin and Stern 2009
;
Neupert et al. 2011
;
Chen and Yang 2012
;
Repellin et al. 2014
, which host fractionalized quasiparticles within the bulk, the elementary bulk excitations of FSPT phases are gapped fermions.
Nevertheless, they host anomalous edge excitations.

In strongly interacting systems, the conventional concept of electronic bands loses relevance.
However, FSPT phases can still be characterized by a rich array of experimental observables, including quantized bulk response functions
Qi et al. 2008
;
Ye and Wang 2013
, and the emergence of intriguing anomalous boundary modes
Lu and Vishwanath 2012
;
Vishwanath and Senthil 2013
.

In the realm of strongly correlated systems, the classification of topological insulators and superconductors takes on a distinct character compared to their non-interacting counterparts.
Interactions can give rise to entirely new topological phases
Wang et al. 2014
, while also leading to the adiabatic connection of certain band topological phases to trivial ones upon their introduction
Fidkowski and Kitaev 2010
;
Fidkowski and Kitaev 2011
;
Turner et al. 2011
.
For instance, in one-dimensional time-reversal invariant spinless superconductors (class BDI
Schnyder et al. 2008
), the number of edge Majorana zero modes characterizes
\mathbb{Z}
free fermion classes.
However, the addition of interactions can generate an energy gap via eight Majorana modes, resulting in a classification of eight distinct classes
Fidkowski and Kitaev 2010
;
Fidkowski and Kitaev 2011
;
Turner et al. 2011
.
Thus, the development of new tools for detecting and classifying interacting fermionic symmetry-protected topological (FSPT) phases becomes imperative.

In recent years, significant progress has been made in this direction.
Field theories have been proposed to explore the quantized response functions of systems coupled to external gauge fields, revealing their stability against interactions
Wang et al. 2014
;
Cheng and Gu 2014
.
While these approaches provide valuable insights into the universal properties of FSPT phases, achieving complete classifications remains a formidable task.
To address this challenge, sophisticated mathematical tools such as group supercohomology
Gu and Wen 2014
and topological field theories incorporating spin structures
Kapustin et al. 2015
;
Gaiotto and Kapustin 2016
have been developed for the classification of FSPT phases.
However, most of these methods focus on systems with finite symmetry groups and are inadequate for capturing the full richness of topological insulators with
U_{f}(1)
charge conservation symmetry.

Notably, exact solvable models have been constructed to realize interacting topological superconductors in two dimensions
Tarantino and Fidkowski 2016
;
Wang et al. 2018
, which were subsequently generalized to two-dimensional topological insulators
Son and Alicea 2019
;
Metlitski 2019
.
These exact solvable models not only provide fixed-point wavefunctions for topological phases but also offer a comprehensive classification of FSPT phases with or without
U_{f}(1)
symmetry
Wang and Gu 2020
;
Wang et al. 2021
.
Such advancements provide invaluable insights into the intricate landscape of interacting topological insulators and highlight the remarkable interplay between topology and strong electronic correlations.

Apart from the important task of classifying FSPT phases, it is also crucial to search for these phases within realistic models using numerical techniques.
In this regard, variational methods serve as widely employed tools in the study of strongly correlated systems.
By parameterizing a subset of the total Hilbert space as the space of variational wavefunctions, ground state wavefunctions can be obtained through energy minimization algorithms within this variational space.
Various observables can then be extracted from the optimized wavefunction, enabling the identification of the quantum phase.
Notable examples of variational methods in strongly correlated models include the variational Monte Carlo method based on Gutzwiller projective wavefunctions, and the density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) method based on matrix product states (MPS)
White 1992
;
White 1993
;
Östlund and Rommer 1995
;
Dukelsky et al. 1998
.

In this work, our focus lies on providing variational wavefunctions for non-chiral fermionic topological insulators in two-dimensions.
In particular, we will use fermionic tensor network states
Barthel et al. 2009
;
Kraus et al. 2010
;
Corboz et al. 2010
;
Gu et al. 2010
;
Schuch et al. 2011
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
;
Bultinck et al. 2017b
;
Wille et al. 2017
;
Cirac et al. 2021
as tools to provide variational wavefunctions.
One of the main advantages of tensor networks lies in their ability to capture complicated quantum entanglement patterns, a key feature of topological phases
Zeng et al. 2019
;
Wen 2019
.
By representing the many-body wavefunction as a network of interconnected tensors, fermionic tensor networks provide an efficient and compact description of topological phases in electronic systems.
Moreover, the variational nature of tensor network methods allows for systematic optimization of wavefunctions within the variational space, enabling the exploration of a wide range of topological phases
Levin and Nave 2007
;
Gu et al. 2008
;
Orús and Vidal 2009
;
Xie et al. 2012
;
Evenbly and Vidal 2015
;
Yang et al. 2017
;
Liao et al. 2019
.
This combination of entanglement representation and variational flexibility makes fermionic tensor networks an ideal choice for studying and characterizing the rich landscape of interacting topological insulators and superconductors.
It is worth noting that the unique properties of topological phases are intricately encoded in their complex entanglement patterns, which are often challenging to extract solely from local observable measurements within the optimized wavefunction.
To overcome this difficulty, one can leverage the classification results of topological phases.
We introduce an innovative approach distinct from the direct energy optimization within the full variational space of fermionic tensors.
Our proposed strategy involves constraining the variational space into distinct subspaces, each associated with a specific topological phase.
By applying optimization algorithms exclusively within each subspace, we can ascertain the quantum phase by comparing the optimized energies across all these subspaces.
This approach allows for a more targeted exploration of the phase diagram, taking advantage of the known classification of topological phases.

Before delving into our specific approach, let us briefly review how tensor network states are employed in the context of bosonic symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases
Chen et al. 2011a
;
Chen et al. 2011b
;
Chen et al. 2012
;
Levin and Gu 2012
;
Senthil and Levin 2013
.
As the name suggests, tensor network states are constructed from small tensors that possess both physical and internal legs.
Physical wavefunctions are obtained by contracting the internal legs while leaving the physical legs untouched.
Although not directly manifest in the physical states, the internal legs encode crucial entanglement information regarding the interplay between topology and symmetry
Schuch et al. 2010
;
Schuch et al. 2011
;
Turner et al. 2011
;
Haegeman et al. 2012
;
Pollmann and Turner 2012
.
By studying the transformation rules of the symmetry group on the internal legs, one can construct and classify SPT phases in various dimensions.
In one-dimensional SPT phases, MPS were initially employed for their classification, with distinct classes corresponding to different projective representations of the symmetry group on the internal legs
Chen et al. 2011a
;
Pollmann and Turner 2012
;
Schuch et al. 2011
.
In two-dimensional SPT phases, projected entangled pair states (PEPS) with symmetry actions on the internal legs represented by inequivalent matrix product operators (MPO) or gauge transformations were shown to capture the classification
Williamson et al. 2017
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
;
Schuch et al. 2010
;
Jiang and Ran 2017a
.
Given the symmetry action on the internal legs, one can then solve for a subspace of local tensors that satisfy the symmetry constraints, thereby obtaining variational tensor wavefunctions.

To construct variational wavefunctions for topological insulators, fermionic tensor network states, characterized by their fermionic legs, are essential
Kraus et al. 2010
;
Bultinck et al. 2017b
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a

In particular, we will employ a specific type of fermionic tensor network state known as fermionic projected entangled-pair states (FPEPS) as our variational ansatz.
In our previous work
Ma et al. 2023
, FPEPS have demonstrated their efficacy in accurately capturing the behavior of interacting quantum spin Hall phases
Wang et al. 2021
.
In this study, instead of focusing on specific examples, we adopt a more general approach.
We start by making certain assumptions about the symmetries associated with the internal legs and derive consistent conditions, which we refer to as tensor equations.
Notably, these tensor equations capture the algebraic data that characterize the quantum anomalies of the edge theories in topological insulators.
By exploring the various sets of tensor equations, we achieve a comprehensive classification of topological insulators, aligning with previous investigations
Wang et al. 2021
.
Additionally, we obtain the symmetry actions on the internal legs by solving the tensor equations.
Subsequently, by imposing these symmetry constraints on the local tensors, we construct the variational spaces corresponding to different topological insulator phases.

Tensor equations offer a compelling avenue for solving variational wavefunctions for topological insulators, serving as a crucial initial step towards their simulation.
Moreover, they provide a novel and insightful description of anomalous edge theories associated with these systems.
This framework allows for the systematic construction of edge Hilbert spaces and the characterization of symmetry actions on edges, shedding light on the intriguing interplay between symmetry and topology in the edge theory.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
II
, we warm up with the FPEPS construction of a topological insulator protected by
U_{f}(1)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
in 2D.
Motivated by exact solvable models, we present the FPEPS representation of the fixed-point wavefunction and extract the tensor equations for symmetry transformation rules on the internal legs of this topological phase.
In Section
III
, we introduce a classification scheme for topological insulators based on anomalous symmetry actions on the edge
Else and Nayak 2014
, which yields consistent results with recent classification proposals based on exactly solvable models
Wang et al. 2021
.
We then develop a general framework for the FPEPS construction of any topological insulator phases in Section
IV
.
By assuming simple forms of symmetries on the internal legs, we derive different sets of tensor equations.
Exhaustively exploring all possible sets of tensor equations allows us to reproduce the previous classification results of topological insulators.
For each set of tensor equations, we provide at least one representative solution, demonstrating the existence of non-empty solution spaces for all topological insulators.
In Section
V
, we extract the edge Hilbert spaces and anomalous edge symmetry actions from these equations, providing concrete realizations of the proposals introduced in Section
III
.
We discuss subtle issues in Section
VI
, where symmetries on internal legs are not parity-even, leading to Kasteleyn orientations, a discrete version of spin structures.
In Section
VII
, we re-examine the examples proposed in Section
II
by solving the constraints on local tensors and thus provide variational wavefunctions beyond the fixed-point.
Finally, in Section
VIII
, we summarize the main findings and discuss future directions.
Technical details and additional mathematical formulations can be found in the Supplementary Material (SM)
10
10

10

See Supplementary Material.

.

II
A simple example – electronic insulators with
U_{f}(1)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
symmetry

In this section, we investigate a specific example of a topological insulator protected by the
U_{f}(1)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
symmetry, characterized by the group relation
g^{2}=e
and
g\cdot n_{f}=n_{f}\cdot g
, where
n_{f}
is the generator for
U_{f}(1)
and
g
is the generator for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
.
This symmetry group gives rise to
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
classes of FSPT phases, and our focus is on the root phase.

We begin by deriving the interacting edge theory using bosonization techniques.
This approach leads us to a decorated-vortex picture for the correlated topological insulator wavefunction, providing insights into the fixed-point wavefunction proposed in previous literature.
Next, we translate the fixed-point wavefunction into a representation using FPEPS, enabling us to extract the symmetry actions on internal legs and derive tensor equations that describe these actions.
It is worth noting that in our previous work
Ma et al. 2023
, we have already explored the FPEPS representation of the quantum spin Hall phase.

For readers interested in the general framework, they can skip this section without affecting their understanding of the subsequent discussions.
We will come back to this example in Section
VII
, where we apply our general framework to analyze this specific example.

II.1
From edge theory to decorated vortices

Similar to the quantum spin Hall phase
Wu et al. 2006
;
Xu and Moore 2006
, the non-interacting edge modes of this phase are described by helical Dirac fermions, whose low-energy Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle H_{\text{edge}}=\int\operatorname{d}\!x\,\operatorname{i}v_{F}\,\Psi^{\dagger}\partial_{x}\tau_{z}\Psi,

(1)

where
\Psi=(\psi_{L},\psi_{R})^{\mathrm{t}}
describes fermion modes with strong spin-orbit coupling, and the right/left movers
\psi_{R/L}
correspond to spin up/down, respectively.
Consequently, the symmetry actions on
\Psi
are given by

\displaystyle U_{f}(\varphi):\Psi\to\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\varphi}\Psi,\quad g:\Psi\to\tau^{z}\Psi,

(2)

which forbid any possible mass terms.

To observe the effect of interactions, we bosonize
Haldane 1981
Eq. (
1
) using the fields
\phi(x)
and
\theta(x)
, both having a periodicity of
2\pi
.
Here, we define
\psi_{R/L}=\exp[-\operatorname{i}(\theta\pm\phi/2)]
, and the commutation relation is given by
[\phi(x),\theta(x^{\prime})]=-2\pi\operatorname{i}\Theta(x-x^{\prime})
, where
\Theta(x)
is a step function.
By comparing with Eq. (
2
), we determine the symmetry actions on the bosonic fields as

\displaystyle U_{f}(\varphi):\theta\to\theta+\varphi,\quad\phi\to\phi,

\displaystyle g:\theta\to\theta+\frac{\pi}{2},\quad\phi\to\phi+\pi,

(3)

respectively.
The Lagrangian density for this interacting edge system is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{4\pi K}\left[\frac{(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}}{v_{F}}-v_{F}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}\right]+\gamma\cos(2\phi-\phi_{0}),

(4)

where
\gamma
and
\phi_{0}
depend on microscopic details.
In the absence of interactions, we have
K=2
for free fermions.
Without loss of generality, we can set
\gamma>0
and
\phi_{0}=0
.
The
\gamma
-term represents the dominant scattering term allowed by symmetry, with scaling dimension
2K
.
For
K>1
, this term is irrelevant, resulting in a gapless system.
However, for
K<1
, the term becomes relevant, driving the system into a gapped phase.
In the limit of large
\gamma
, this gapped phase exhibits two degenerate ground states characterized by
\langle\phi\rangle=0
or
\langle\phi\rangle=\pi
.
According to Eq. (
3
), these ground states are transformed into each other by the action of
g
, leading to the identification of this gapped phase as a spontaneous
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
symmetry-breaking phase.

Furthermore, this symmetry-breaking edge phase exhibits anomalous behavior, which can be observed in the properties of its domain walls.
To illustrate this, let us consider a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
domain wall located at
x_{0}
, where
\langle\phi\rangle=0
for
x<x_{0}-\varepsilon
and
\langle\phi\rangle=\pi
for
x>x_{0}+\varepsilon
, as depicted in Fig.
1
.
In the language of bosonization, the charge density operator is given by
\rho=-\partial_{x}\phi/2\pi
, and thus the total charge
Q=\pm\frac{1}{2}
is enclosed around the domain wall, known as the Goldstone-Wilczek half soliton
Goldstone and Wilczek 1981
:

\displaystyle Q=\int_{x_{0}-\varepsilon}^{x_{0}+\varepsilon}-\operatorname{d}\!x\,\frac{\partial_{x}\phi}{2\pi}=\pm\frac{1}{2},

(5)

where the
\pm
signs correspond to counterclockwise and clockwise rotations, respectively.

To establish a connection with spin degrees of freedom, we can map the compact bosonic field
\phi
to spin using
S^{+}=\exp(\operatorname{i}\phi)
.
In this mapping,
\phi=0
corresponds to ”spin up” and
\phi=\pi
corresponds to ”spin down”.

Figure 1:
Schematic plot of the edge soliton and bulk vortex.
Gray dashed line denotes the edge of a 2D system.
Configurations of
Q=\pm\frac{1}{2}
on the edge correspond to clockwise/counter-clockwise rotating
\phi
along the edge.
We extend edge field
\phi
to the bulk, where a solition now is interpreted as “half” vortex(
n_{v}=1
)/anti-vortex(
n_{v}=-1
).
So, a vortex/anti-vortex contains
\pm 1
charge.

As illustrated in Fig.
1
, we extend the edge field
\phi
into the bulk, where an edge domain wall can be viewed as “half” of a bulk vortex (
n_{v}=1
) or anti-vortex (
n_{v}=-1
), depending on its rotation direction
Chen et al. 2014
;
Liu et al. 2014
.
As an edge soliton carries
Q=\pm\frac{1}{2}
charge, it is natural to associate vortices with
U_{f}(1)
charge, with
n_{f}=n_{v}
where
n_{v}
labels the number of vortices.

The anomalous nature of the edge theory can also be understood through the fusion rules of symmetry flux.
Acting
g
on a subregion
M
of the edge, where
\phi\to\phi+\pi
within
M
, creates
g
-flux at the ends of
M
.
If we apply the
g
action twice within
M
, treating it as a continuous process in time, the current operator
j=\Psi^{\dagger}\tau_{z}\Psi=\partial_{t}\phi/2\pi
indicates the presence of a current within
M
, resulting in the accumulation of unit charges at the two ends of
M
.
In other words, the fusion of two
g
-flux leads to unit fermions, which is impossible in a conventional 1D system.

II.2
The fixed-point wavefunction and FPEPS construction

II.2.1
The fixed-point wavefunction on a honeycomb lattice

Motivated by the decorated vortex picture derived in the above subsection, we introduce the fixed-point wavefunction of the interacting topological insulator phase protected by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}\times U_{f}(1)
.
As shown in Fig.
2
, the system consists of two types of local degree of freedom: spinless fermions
f
at a honeycomb lattice and Ising spins
\lvert\tau\rangle
at the plaquette centers of this lattice, forming a dual triangular lattice.

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
rotates Ising spins, while acts trivially on fermions:

\displaystyle g:f\to f\,,~\lvert\uparrow\rangle\leftrightarrow\lvert\downarrow\rangle\,.

(6)

In other words, the Ising spin mimics
\phi
-field, which rotates by
\pm\pi
when crossing an Ising domain wall.
To distinguish between
\pm\pi
rotation, arrows are added on the bonds of the dual lattice, and
\phi
rotate
\pm\pi
when crossing an Ising domain wall along/against the arrow.
In the arrow convention in Fig.
2
,
n_{v}=\pm 1
lives at site with sublattice index
u/v
whenever an Ising domain wall passes through this site.
To match the decorated vortex picture where
n_{f}=n_{v}
, fermions at sublattice
u/v
are set to be electrons/holes:

\displaystyle[n_{f;s},f_{s}]=-(-1)^{s}f_{s}~,

(7)

where
(-1)^{s}=\pm 1
for
s=u/v
.
Namely,
n_{f;s}=(-1)^{s}f_{s}^{\dagger}f_{s}
.

Figure 2:
A typical configuration for the fixed-point wavefunction of the topological insulator phase.
Spinless fermion
f
lives at a honeycomb lattice, while
\lvert\tau\rangle
resides at the dual triangular lattice.
Spin up/down is colored blue/red.
When crossing an Ising domain wall (colored yellow) along/against oriented bonds of the dual lattice, One adds
\pm\pi
.
The arrows indicate the convention: when a domain wall crosses a site with sublattice index
u/v
, a vortex/anti-vortex decorated with a hole/electron appears at this site.

Fig.
2
shows a typical decorated vortex configurations with fermions.
The fixed-point wavefunction can be expressed as superposition of all such configurations:

\displaystyle\lvert\Psi\rangle=\sum_{c}\Psi(c)\lvert c\rangle\otimes\lvert\psi_{c}\rangle~,

(8)

where
\lvert c\rangle
is a product state of Ising spins and
\lvert\psi_{c}\rangle
is the fermion decoration.
The sum is taken over all Ising spin configurations.
Here,
\left\lvert\Psi(c)\right\rvert=1
, and fermion ordering are fixed to ensure that there is no sign ambiguity for
\Psi(c)
.
It is important to note that on any domain wall loop, there are always the same number of
u-
and
v-
sites.
Thus, according to Eq. (
7
),
\lvert\psi_{c}\rangle
has zero
U_{f}(1)
charge, making
\lvert\Psi\rangle

U_{f}(1)
symmetric.
Additionally, to ensure
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
symmetry of
\lvert\Psi\rangle
, we require
\Psi(c)=\Psi(g\circ c)

2
2

2

The specific form of
\Phi(c)
depend on the ordering of fermions on sites and can be quite complicated.
However, for the purpose of this discussion here, we do not need to consider the detailed forms of
\Phi(c)
’s.

.

II.2.2
FPEPS representation

We now proceed to construct the FPEPS representation of the fixed-point wavefunction on the honeycomb lattice.
FPEPS are formed using fermionic tensors, which are quantum states in the fermionic tensor product (
\otimes_{f}
) of physical and internal legs.
Legs with inward/outward arrows represent fermionic Hilbert spaces for ket/bra states.
The connection between outward and inward internal legs involves contracting states using the
{\rm{fTr}}
operation, defined as

\displaystyle{\rm{fTr}}[\langle i\rvert\otimes_{f}\lvert j\rangle]=(-)^{\left\lvert i\right\rvert\left\lvert j\right\rvert}{\rm{fTr}}[\lvert j\rangle\otimes_{f}\langle i\rvert]=\delta_{ij}

(9)

where
(-1)^{\left\lvert i\right\rvert}
denotes the fermion parity of the state
\lvert i\rangle/\langle i\rvert
, with
\left\lvert i\right\rvert\in{0,1}
.
Physical wavefunctions for FPEPS are obtained by contracting all internal legs.
Throughout this paper, all local tensors are assumed to be
parity even
.
More details on fermionic tensor networks are presented in Sec.I of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

By imposing translation symmetry on the honeycomb lattice, we introduce two types of site tensors (
\hat{T}_{u,v}
) and three types of bond tensors (
\hat{B}_{x,y,z}
), as illustrated in Fig.
3
.
Each site tensor supports a physical fermion, while each bond tensor accommodates two physical Ising spins.
The Ising spins within a plaquette are set to be the same, effectively representing the plaquette spins.

The internal leg
(s\alpha)
extends from
\hat{B}_{\alpha}
to
\hat{T}_{s}
and is represented as a triple line in Fig.
3
.
The middle line corresponds to a spinless fermion
c_{(s\alpha)}
with charge
(-1)^{1-s}
, while the lines on the sides carry two internal Ising spins.
Thus, the basis states for the internal leg
(s\alpha)
are
(c_{(s\alpha)}^{\dagger})^{n_{\alpha}}\left|0\right){(s\alpha)}\left|\tau\beta\tau_{\gamma}\right){(s\alpha)}
, where the ordering of
\tau{\beta}\tau_{\gamma}
follows the direction of the dashed arcs in Fig.
3
.

As all spins within a plaquette are identical, a typical state for site tensor
\hat{T}_{u}
is given by

\displaystyle(c_{(ux)}^{\dagger})^{n_{x}}(c_{(uy)}^{\dagger})^{n_{y}}(c_{(uz)}^{\dagger})^{n_{z}}\left|0\right)\left|\tau_{z}\tau_{y}\right)_{(ux)}\left|\tau_{x}\tau_{z}\right)_{(uy)}\left|\tau_{y}\tau_{x}\right)_{(uz)}

\displaystyle\otimes_{f}(f_{u}^{\dagger})^{n}\lvert 0\rangle

(10)

which can be conveniently expressed as
\left|\tau_{x}\tau_{y}\tau_{z};\,n_{x}n_{y}n_{z}\right)\lvert n\rangle
.
Similar notation applies to typical states in
\hat{T}_{v}
.

Figure 3:
Illustration of basis states for site tensors.
Physical fermions locate at the site center (green circle), where the
\pm
signs label
U_{f}(1)
charges.
Each internal leg is represented by three lines, where the two side lines are plaquette Ising spins, and the middle line gives an internal fermion.

By employing the decorated vortex construction discussed earlier, a site accommodates one fermion whenever an Ising domain wall intersects the site.
Additionally, in order to ensure that the physical wavefunction carries zero
U_{f}(1)
charge, all local tensors are designed to be charge-neutral.
To fulfill these requirements, for the internal leg
(s\alpha)
, we can simply introduce a fermion mode
c^{\dagger}{(s\alpha)}\left|0\right)
for the state
\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right)
, while keeping zero fermions for the other three spin states
\left|\uparrow\uparrow\right)
,
\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right)
, and
\left|\downarrow\downarrow\right)
.
Here,
c{(s\alpha)}
carries the opposite charge compared to
f_{s}
.
Thus, in comparison with Eq. (
7
), we have

\displaystyle[n_{f;(s\alpha)},c_{(s\alpha)}]=(-1)^{s}c_{(s\alpha)},

(11)

which implies that
n_{f;(s\alpha)}=(-1)^{s+1}c_{(s\alpha)}^{\dagger}c_{(s\alpha)}
.

Using the concise notation, we assert that the site tensors for the fixed-point wavefunction can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{u}={}

\displaystyle\big[\left|\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\right)+\left|\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\right)\big]\lvert 0\rangle+\big[\operatorname{i}\left|\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow;001\right)+\left|\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow;100\right)+\left|\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow;001\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{i}\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow;010\right)+\operatorname{i}\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow;100\right)+\left|\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow;010\right)\big]\lvert 1\rangle

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{v}={}

\displaystyle\big[\left|\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\right)+\left|\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\right)\big]\lvert 0\rangle+\big[\left|\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow;001\right)+\operatorname{i}\left|\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow;100\right)+\operatorname{i}\left|\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow;001\right)

\displaystyle+\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow;010\right)+\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow;100\right)+\operatorname{i}\left|\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow;010\right)\big]\lvert 1\rangle

(12)

which can be represented graphically as Fig.
4
:

Figure 4:
Illustration of site tensors.

Coefficients of site tensors are chosen according to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
symmetry, as will be discussed in Section
II.2.3
.

Due to the constraints where all Ising spins within a plaquette are identical, a typical state for bond tensor
\hat{B}_{\alpha}
reads

\displaystyle\langle\tau_{0}\tau_{1}\rvert_{\alpha}\otimes_{f}\left(\tau_{0}\tau_{1}\right|_{(u\alpha)}\left(\tau_{0}\tau_{1}\right|_{(v\alpha)}(c_{(u\alpha)})^{n_{u}}(c_{(v\alpha)})^{n_{v}}~,

(13)

which can be shorten as
\langle\tau_{0}\tau_{1};n_{u}n_{v}\rvert_{\alpha}
.
Here, the order of
\tau
’s on leg
(s\alpha)
follows arrows in dashed arcs in Fig.
3
.
In this work, bond tensors are set to be maximal entangled states as

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\alpha}=\langle\uparrow\uparrow;00\rvert_{\alpha}+\langle\downarrow\downarrow;00\rvert_{\alpha}+\langle\uparrow\downarrow;00\rvert_{\alpha}-\langle\downarrow\uparrow;11\rvert_{\alpha}

(14)

Such tensors are apparently charge neutral.
Note that to match internal states in site tensors, only
\langle\downarrow\uparrow\rvert
is accompanied with a fermion mode.
The
\pm
signs of bond tensor entries are important for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
symmetry, and will be discussed in Section
II.2.3
.

II.2.3
Equivalence between the fixed-point wavefunction and the FPEPS representation

In this part, we demonstrate that by contracting all internal legs, the FPEPS construction presented in the previous part yields the fixed-point wavefunction.
The physical wavefunction reads
\lvert\Psi\rangle={\rm{fTr}}\left[\mathbb{T}\otimes_{f}\mathbb{B}\right]
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=\bigotimes_{\bm{\mathbf{r}}s}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}\hat{T}_{(\bm{\mathbf{r}}s)}~,\quad\mathbb{B}=\bigotimes_{\bm{\mathbf{r}}\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}\hat{B}_{\bm{\mathbf{r}}\alpha}~,

(15)

where
\bm{\mathbf{r}}
is coordinate for unit cell, and entries of
\hat{T}_{(\bm{\mathbf{r}}s)}
and
\hat{B}_{\bm{\mathbf{r}}\alpha}
follow Eq. (
12
) and Eq. (
14
) respectively.
The ordering of tensors is not important as all local tensors are parity even.

\lvert\Psi\rangle
can be organized according to plaquette Ising spin configurations
c
, as in Eq. (
8
).
In the following, we will show that such tensor wavefunction shares the same properties as the fixed-point wavefunction: for all
c
,
\left\lvert\Psi(c)\right\rvert=1
and
\Psi(c)=\Psi(g\circ c)
.

Given an Ising spin configuration
c
, by overlapping
\lvert\Psi\rangle
with
\lvert c\rangle
, we obtain
\Psi(c)\lvert\psi(c)\rangle
, which is a new tensor network state formed by site tensors
\hat{T}_{(\bm{\mathbf{r}}s)}^{c}
and bond tensors
\hat{B}^{c}_{\bm{\mathbf{r}}\alpha}
.
For such a new tensor network, the internal legs are all one-dimensional Hilbert spaces with either bosonic or fermionic internal states.
Since the entries of site and bond tensors have unit modulus, we conclude that
\left\lvert\Psi(c)\right\rvert=1
.

To show
\Psi(c)=\Psi(g\circ c)
for arbitrary spin configurations, we discuss three cases separately.

1.

For the trivial case where all Ising spins points up/down, it is easy to see that
\Psi(c)=\Psi(g\circ c)=1
.

2.

Consider a configuration
c
characterized by a single domain wall loop, where the inside and outside of the loop are labeled as
\downarrow
and
\uparrow
, respectively.
In this case, the configuration
g\circ c
also results in a single domain wall loop, but with the inside and outside regions interchanged, i.e.,
\uparrow
inside and
\downarrow
outside.
The honeycomb lattice’s bipartite nature ensures that the loop contains an equal number of
u
-sites and
v
-sites, which we label as
N
.
Consequently, the loop consists of
2N
bonds.
Tensors away from the loop correspond to pure spin states with a coefficient of
1
, so all the subtleties arise from the tensors on the loop.
We assign labels
\hat{T}_{j}^{c}
to the site tensors along the loop and
\hat{B}^{c}_{j,j+1}
to the bond tensors connecting sites
j
and
j+1
, where
j=\{1,\cdots,2N\}
with an arbitrary starting point, proceeding clockwise along the loop.
Without loss of generality, we choose site
j=1
to be a
u
-site.
The tensor network state
\lvert\psi(c)\rangle
is depicted schematically as follows:

\displaystyle\Psi(c)\lvert\psi(c)\rangle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}~,

(16)

where only tensors in the domain wall loop are presented.
The blue legs are physical fermions, the light gray legs are internal spins connecting to tensors within domains, and the yellow dots represent fermion swapping gates.
The internal legs traveling from right to left are colored red and introduce an additional factor of
-1
when contracting fermionic states according to Eq. (3) in Sec.I of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.
The tensor network state
\Psi(g\circ c)\lvert\psi(g\circ c)\rangle
can be graphically represented in a similar manner.
The coefficients for these two configurations come from two contributions: fermion swapping and tensor entries.

From Eq. (
14
), the tensor
\hat{B}_{2k-1,2k}^{c}
contains fermion modes
\left(0\right|c_{2k-1}
and
\left(0\right|c_{2k}
, while
\hat{B}_{2k,2k+1}^{c}
carries zero fermion charge.
Contracting
\hat{B}_{2k-1,2k}^{c}
with
\hat{T}_{2k-1}^{c}
and
\hat{T}_{2k}^{c}
, and
\hat{B}_{2k,2k+1}^{c}
with
\hat{T}_{2k}^{c}
and
\hat{T}_{2k+1}^{c}
both yield a factor of
-1
, resulting in no sign factor overall.
For the configuration
g\circ c
with an
\uparrow
domain inside the loop,
\hat{B}_{2k,2k+1}^{g\circ c}
contains fermion modes
\left(0\right|c_{2k}
and
\left(0\right|c_{2k+1}
, while
\hat{B}_{2k-1,2k}^{g\circ c}
is a pure spin state.
Contracting
\hat{B}_{2k,2k+1}^{g\circ c}
with
\hat{T}_{2k}^{g\circ c}
and
\hat{T}_{2k+1}^{g\circ c}
for
k<N
yields a factor of
-1
, and contracting
\hat{B}_{2N,1}^{g\circ c}
with
\hat{T}_{1}^{g\circ c}
and
\hat{T}_{2N}^{g\circ c}
does not introduce a phase factor.
Thus, the phase difference between
\Psi(c)
and
\Psi(g\circ c)
due to fermion swapping for a single loop with
2N
sites is
(-)^{N-1}
.

We now analyze the contributions from tensor entries.
According to Eq. (
14
), entries of
\hat{B}_{j,j+1}^{c}
and
\hat{B}_{j,j+1}^{g\circ c}
have opposite signs for all
j
, implying that the
2N
bonds contribute no phase difference.
We consider the sites on a domain wall loop, which can be classified into two types based on their angles:
\pm\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites.
For loops with
2N
sites, the number of
\pm\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites is equal to
N\pm 3
.
To see this, let us consider a domain wall loop with
2N
sites and
N_{+}

\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites.
Expanding such a loop by adding a unit honeycomb loop, the two
\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites on the original loop transform into
-\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites.
We assume that
n_{-}

-\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites (where
0\leq n_{-}\leq 4
) on the original loop become sites in the domain.
After expansion, the half number of total sites (
N
) as well as the number of
\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites both increase by
(2-n_{-})
.
Considering that a unit honeycomb loop has
N=3
and
N_{+}=N+3=6
, and any loop can be expanded from a unit honeycomb loop, we conclude that there are
(N+3)

\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites and
(N-3)

-\frac{2\pi}{3}
-sites.
An example is shown in Fig.
5
.

Figure 5:
An example of the inductive proof.
Before adding a new plaquette to the loop in the left figure, there are 8
N_{+}
sites and 2
N_{-}
sites, and after adding a new plaquette to the loop, one site is packed into the domain (
n_{-}=1
), then both
N_{+}
and
N_{-}
increase by
1=2-n_{-}
.

Let the number of
\pm\frac{2\pi}{3}
-
s
-sites be
N_{s\pm}
.
Since the number of
u
-sites is equal to the number of
v
-sites on the loop, we have
N_{u-}=N-N_{u+}
,
N_{v+}=N+3-N_{u+}
, and
N_{v-}=N_{u+}-3
.
From Eq. (
12
), we conclude that the phase difference between configurations
c
and
g\circ c
due to entries of site tensors is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{i}^{N_{u+}+N_{v-}}\cdot(-\operatorname{i})^{N_{u-}+N_{v+}}=(-1)^{N-1}.

(17)

By considering the contributions from fermion swapping and tensor entries, we can conclude that
\Psi(c)=\Psi(g\circ c)
for
c
with a single domain wall.

3.

For configurations with multiple domain wall loops, one can move tensors belonging to each individual loop together.
The analysis presented above can then be applied to each loop separately, leading to the conclusion that
\Phi(g\circ c)=\Phi(c)
is always satisfied.

In conclusion, for any configuration
c
with
N_{dw}(c)
domain wall loops, we have
\Psi(c)=\Psi(g\circ c)
, and thus
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
symmetric.

II.3
Tensor equations from the fixed-point wavefunction

In this work, we make the assumption that symmetries act as gauge transformations on internal legs, which are canceled out during tensor contraction, resulting in symmetric physical wavefunctions.
These gauge transformations, in general, do not satisfy the same group relations as physical symmetries in topological phases.
The relations between these gauge transformations are characterized by tensor equations.
Please see more details in Section
IV.1
and Sec.I of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

Tensor equations play a crucial role in our work.
They serve two main purposes: First, they provide algebraic data that characterizes the anomalous edge theory of FSPT phases.
Second, solutions to tensor equations yield symmetry actions on internal legs of local tensors, which can be imposed to obtain variational tensor wavefunctions for FSPT phases.
In this subsection, we introduce tensor equations by examining the symmetries of local fermionic tensors constructed in the previous subsection.
The tensor equations for generic topological insulators will be discussed in Section
IV
.

Let’s begin by considering the
U_{f}(1)
symmetry with the charge operator
n_{f}
.
From Eq.(
7
) and Eq.(
11
), it is easy to verify that the local tensors in Eq.(
12
) and Eq.(
14
) are charge neutral:

\displaystyle\left(n_{f;s}+\sum_{\alpha}n_{f;(s\alpha)}\right)\cdot\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\alpha}\cdot\left(n_{f;(u\alpha)}+n_{f;(v\alpha)}\right)

\displaystyle=0~,.

(18)

Thus, the physical wavefunction obtained by contracting internal legs is also charge neutral.

Next, let’s consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
symmetry acting on local tensors.
It can be observed that the local tensors in Eq. (
12
) and (
14
) satisfy the following equations:

\displaystyle W_{(sx)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(sy)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(sz)}(g)\cdot\hat{T}_{s}=\hat{T}_{s}\,,

(19)

\displaystyle V_{(\alpha 1)}(g)\otimes V_{(\alpha 2)}(g)\cdot\hat{B}_{\alpha}\cdot W^{-1}_{(u\alpha)}(g)\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{(v\alpha)}(g)=\hat{B}_{\alpha}\,,

where
V_{(\alpha 1/2)}(g)
are
g
action on physical Ising spin
\tau_{0}/\tau_{1}
defined in Eq. (
6
).

W(g)
’s are
g
-action on internal legs, which read

\displaystyle W_{(sx)}(g)=W_{(sz)}(g)

\displaystyle=\left|\uparrow\uparrow\right)\left(\downarrow\downarrow\right|+\left|\downarrow\uparrow,1\right)\left(\uparrow\downarrow\right|+\operatorname{i}\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right)\left(\downarrow\uparrow,1\right|+\left|\downarrow\downarrow\right)\left(\uparrow\uparrow\right|\,,

\displaystyle W_{(sy)}(g)

\displaystyle=\left|\uparrow\uparrow\right)\left(\downarrow\downarrow\right|-\left|\downarrow\uparrow,1\right)\left(\uparrow\downarrow\right|-\operatorname{i}\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right)\left(\downarrow\uparrow,1\right|+\left|\downarrow\downarrow\right)\left(\uparrow\uparrow\right|\,.

(20)

It is important to note that
W(g)
’s do not have fixed parity.
Eq. (
19
) does not imply a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
-symmetric physical wavefunction, as swapping
W(g)
’s introduces additional fermion swapping gates that may break the cancellation between
W(g)
and
W^{-1}(g)
.
However, as we will show later in Section
VI
, Eq. (
19
) leads to the Kasteleyn orientation, which in turn yields a global
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{Ising}}
symmetry.

We now explore the group relation between
n_{f}
and
g
on internal legs.
While they commute with each other on physical legs,
W(g)
’s do not commute with
n_{f}
, instead satisfying

\displaystyle W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\circ n_{f;(s\alpha)}=n_{f;(s\alpha)}+n_{D;(s\alpha)}

(21)

Here, we use the notation “
A\circ B
” to represent
A\cdot B\cdot A^{-1}
.
The operator
n_{D}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle n_{D;(s\alpha)}\equiv(-1)^{s+1}\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right)\left(\uparrow\downarrow\right|+(-1)^{s}\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right)\left(\downarrow\uparrow\right|~,

(22)

n_{D}
’s are also “symmetry charges” for local tensors, but they act trivially on physical legs:

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\alpha}n_{D;(s\alpha)}\right)\cdot\hat{T}_{s}=0,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\alpha}\cdot\left(n_{D;(u\alpha)}+n_{D;(v\alpha)}\right)=0,.

(23)

These internal leg symmetries also form a group, referred to as the invariant gauge group (IGG)
Wen 7071
;
Jiang and Ran 2015
;
Jiang and Ran 2017b
.
In cases where the IGG is a Lie group, its Lie algebra is called the invariant gauge algebra (IGA).
To ensure a symmetric physical wavefunction, the group relations on internal legs may differ from those on physical legs by an arbitrary IGG element.
Roughly speaking, the IGG can be seen as identity elements on internal legs.

Physically,
n_{D;(s\alpha)}
contains information about domain walls on the internal leg
(s\alpha)
: it returns
0
for configurations without domain walls, while returning
\pm 1
when domain walls are present.
It is worth mentioning that
n_{D}
’s can be further decomposed into ”plaquette IGA”
n_{\lambda}
’s:

\displaystyle n_{D;(s\alpha)}=n_{\lambda;(s\alpha 1)}+n_{\lambda;(s\alpha 2)}

(24)

where

\displaystyle n_{\lambda;(s\alpha a)}=(-1)^{a+1}\left|\downarrow\right)_{(s\alpha a)}\left(\downarrow\right|_{(s\alpha a)},,~a\in{1,2}

(25)

Here,
\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right)_{(s\alpha a)}
denotes the
a
th Ising spin on the internal leg
(s\alpha)
, with
a=1,2
ordered counter-clockwise for both sites:

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\,,

(26)

As mentioned,
n_{\lambda}
’s form a “plaquette IGA”, meaning that local tensors vanish when acting
n_{\lambda}
’s on legs within a plaquette:

\displaystyle(n_{\lambda;(s,\alpha,1)}+n_{\lambda;(s,\alpha+1,2)})\cdot\hat{T}_{s}=0\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\alpha}\cdot(n_{\lambda;(s\alpha 1)}+n_{\lambda;(s\alpha 2)})=0\,.

(27)

We point out that another group relation
g^{2}=e
also no longer holds on internal legs.
Instead, we have

\displaystyle\exp\left[\operatorname{i}\frac{\pi}{2}n_{D;(s\alpha)}^{2}\right]\cdot\left[W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\right]^{2}=\hat{1}

(28)

Such complicated group relation will be explained in Section
IV
and Section
VII
.

To complete the tensor equations, we also consider the nontrivial symmetry action on IGG elements.
It is easy to observe that
W(g)
anticommutes with
n_{D}
:

\displaystyle\{W_{(s\alpha)}(g),n_{D;(s\alpha)}\}=0.

(29)

Regarding
W(g)
’s action on
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\circ n_{\lambda;(s\alpha a)}=-n_{\lambda;(s\alpha a)}+(-1)^{a}.

(30)

In summary, we can group the equations obtained from the FPEPS construction of the fixed-point wavefunction into three categories:

1.

Eq. (
21
), (
24
), (
28
), (
29
), and (
30
) are referred to as
tensor equations
, representing the “group relations” of the symmetry group and IGG on the internal legs of FPEPS.

2.

Eq. (
20
), (
22
), and (
25
) provide one solution to these tensor equations, forming a representation of the symmetry group and IGG on internal legs.

3.

Once obtaining a representation, we impose constraints on the local tensors as given in Eq. (
18
), (
19
), (
23
), and (
27
).
By solving these constraints, we obtain a variational space of tensor wavefunctions for the given topological insulator phase.

While these equations are derived from the fixed-point wavefunction, it is important to verify whether they truly characterize the topological insulator phase.
In Section
V
, we address this concern by analyzing the anomalous edge theory using these tensor equations.
We demonstrate that they indeed characterize the topological insulator phase, so solutions obtained by methods in Item
3
indeed give variational wavefunctions beyond the fixed-point.

III
Classification of topological insulators through symmetry action on edge

Topological insulators are renowned for their metallic edge states, which are protected by the anomalous symmetry actions when confined to the edge.
This anomalous symmetry action can be related to the anomalous fusion rules of symmetry defects, as discussed in Ref.
Else and Nayak 2014
and
Metlitski 2019
.
In this section, by exploring the potential anomalous fusion rules of symmetry defects, we extract the algebraic data for a complete classification 2+1D topological insulators
Wang et al. 2021
.

III.1
Symmetry group for electronic insulators

Let us first give a general description for symmetry group of insulators.
For an electronic insulator, its symmetry group
G_{f}
can generally be expressed as an extended group:

G_{f}=U_{f}(1)\rtimes_{\tilde{\rho},\alpha}G_{b},

(31)

where
\tilde{\rho}:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\alpha:G\times G\to U_{f}(1)
characterize the group extension.
A group element is represented as
(g,\theta)
, with
g\in G_{b}
, and
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
.
The group multiplication rule is given by

(g_{1},\theta_{1})\cdot(g_{2},\theta_{2})=(g_{1}g_{2},\theta_{1}+(-)^{\tilde{\rho}(g_{1})}\theta_{2}+\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\pmod{2}\pi)\,.

(32)

From associativity, we derive the following relations:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(g_{1})+\tilde{\rho}(g_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\rho}(g_{1}g_{2})\,,

(33)

\displaystyle\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})+\alpha(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle=(-)^{\tilde{\rho}(g_{1})}\cdot\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})+\alpha(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})\,.

In other words,
\tilde{\rho}\in H^{1}(G_{b},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
\alpha\in H^{2}_{\widetilde{\rho}}(G_{b},U_{f}(1))
.
One can choose gauge such that
\alpha(e,g)=\alpha(g,e)=0
, and then from Eq. (
32
), we have

\displaystyle(g,0)\cdot(e,\theta)=(e,(-)^{\tilde{\rho}(g)}\theta)\cdot(g,0)

(34)

We introduce another index
s\in H^{1}(G_{b},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
:
s(g)=0/1
if
g\in G_{b}
is a unitary/anti-unitary symmetry action.
Let
n_{f}
be the generator of
U_{f}(1)
, Eq. (
34
) is equivalent to

g\cdot n_{f}\cdot g^{-1}=(-)^{\rho(g)}n_{f}\,,

(35)

where
\rho(g)\equiv\tilde{\rho}(g)+s(g)
.
Namely,
g
is a particle-hole symmetry if
\rho(g)=1
.

By setting
\theta_{1,2}=0
in Eq. (
32
), we have

\displaystyle(g_{1},0)\cdot(g_{2},0)=(g_{1}g_{2},\alpha(g_{1},g_{2}))\,.

(36)

It is easy to verify that Eq. (
35
) (or Eq. (
34
)) and (
36
) give Eq. (
32
).

III.2
Fermionic three-cocycle from edge theory

For an electronic system with Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}=\bigotimes_{f,i}\mathbb{H}_{i}
, the action of
n_{f}
and
g\in G_{b}
on
\mathbb{H}
is given by

\displaystyle n_{f}\equiv\sum_{i}n_{f;i}\,,\quad U(g)K^{s(g)}\equiv\bigotimes_{i}U_{i}(g)K^{s(g)}\,,

(37)

where
n_{f;i}
and
U_{i}(g)
act on the local Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}_{i}
.
Note that in this work, we focus on onsite symmetries.

For a short-range entangled (SRE) phase on a finite-size sample
A
, its low-energy eigenstates are identified as edge states.
Following Ref.
Else and Nayak 2014
, we assume that these edge states can be constructed from a superposition of certain Wannier orbitals localized near the boundary.
In other words, the edge Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
has a tensor product structure:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{edge}\equiv\bigotimes_{j\in\partial A}\mathbb{H}_{j}

(38)

We mention that edge Wannier orbitals are, in general, different from the original local Hilbert spaces.
However, this difference does not affect our analysis in the following.

Now, we examine the symmetry actions projected on
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
, which may be anomalous when the edge is regarded as a (1+1)D system.
These actions are not necessarily onsite.
Here, we consider the case where elements in
U_{f}(1)
act as onsite symmetries on the edge, namely,

\displaystyle n_{f;edge}=\sum_{j\in\partial A}n_{f;j}

(39)

For
g\in G_{b}
, its action on the edge is labeled as
U_{edge}(g)K^{s(g)}
, where
U_{edge}(g)
is assumed to be a finite-depth local unitary.
Note that such action satisfies the group relation defined in Eq. (
32
) and Eq. (
35
):

\displaystyle U_{edge}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot n_{f}\cdot[U_{edge}(g)K^{s(g)}]^{-1}=(-)^{\rho(g)}\cdot n_{f}\,,

\displaystyle U_{edge}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{edge}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot n_{f}\right]\cdot U_{edge}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\,.

(40)

where we use
n_{f}
as a short-hand for
n_{f;edge}
.

Since
U_{\text{edge}}(g)
is of finite depth, it can be truncated to a subregion
M\subset\partial A
, denoted as
U_{M}(g)
.
We require the truncation result to be charge neutral, satisfying

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{a})K^{s(g_{a})}\cdot n_{f}\cdot[U_{M}(g_{a})K^{s(g_{a})}]^{-1}=

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{a})}\cdot n_{f}

(41)

When restricted to
M
, the group relation becomes:

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{M}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}=

\displaystyle\Omega(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\exp\left[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot n_{f}\right]\cdot U_{M}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}

(42)

where
\Omega(g_{1},g_{2})=\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})
, and
\Omega_{a/b}(g_{1},g_{2})
are unitary operators located at
\partial M=\{a,b\}
, which are interpreted as local excitations at ends of
M
.
Note that such decomposition is defined up to a
U(1)
phase factor, as
\Omega(g_{1},g_{2})=(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta}\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2}))\cdot(\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\theta}\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2}))
.

From Eq. (
41
) and (
42
), we have

\displaystyle[n_{f},\Omega(g_{1},g_{2})]=0

(43)

Thus,
\Omega_{a/b}
can carry opposite
U_{f}(1)
quantum number:

[n_{f},\Omega_{a/b}(g_{1},g_{2})]=\mp\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{a/b}(g_{1},g_{2})\,,

(44)

where
\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By definition,
\Omega_{a/b}(g_{1},g_{2})
are operators with even/odd fermion parity iff
\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})
is even/odd.

From associativity and Eq. (
33
), we have

\displaystyle\Omega(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})={}^{U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\Omega(g_{2},g_{3})\Omega(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})\,.

(45)

where
{}^{U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\Omega\equiv U_{M}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot\Omega\cdot K^{s(g)}U_{M}^{-1}(g)
.
As the decomposition of
\Omega
to
\Omega_{a}\cdot\Omega_{b}
is unique up to a
U(1)
phase, we conclude

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{a}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle=(-)^{\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\sigma(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})+\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})}\cdot\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\cdot{}^{U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\Omega_{a}(g_{2},g_{3})\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{b}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle=\omega^{-1}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\cdot{}^{U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\Omega_{b}(g_{2},g_{3})\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})\,,

(46)

where
(-)^{\sigma\sigma}
factors come from exchanging
\Omega_{a}
and
\Omega_{b}
.
Thus, the anomalous symmetry action are characterized by two pieces of data
\sigma
and
\omega
, which constitutes the fermionic 3-cocycle in the presence of
U_{f}(1)
symmetry.
By calculating commutators between
n_{f}
and two sides of the equations in Eq. (
46
), we conclude that
\sigma\in H^{2}_{\rho}(G_{b},\mathbb{Z})
.
Namely,

\displaystyle\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})+\sigma(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=(-)^{\rho(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})+\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

(47)

Consistent conditions for
\omega
’s are given by

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\omega(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3},g_{4})[\omega(g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})]^{1-2s(g_{1})}}{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}g_{4})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\exp\left[\operatorname{i}(-)^{\rho(g_{1}g_{2})}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\sigma(g_{3},g_{4})\right]\cdot(-)^{\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\sigma(g_{3},g_{4})}\,.

(48)

which can be derived by calculating
\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{a}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})\Omega_{a}(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3},g_{4})
in two different ways, where details are shown in Sec.II of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

One may initially expect that distinct solutions of Eq. (
47
) and (
48
) correspond to different edge anomalies.
However, as we will demonstrate in the following subsection, there exist redundancies arising from gauge choices of
U_{M}(g)
and
\Omega_{a/b}(g)
.
To obtain the correct classification, these redundancies must be factored out.

III.3
The coboundary condition

It is important to note that the restriction of
U_{edge}(g)K^{s(g)}
to region
M
is not unique:
U_{M}(g)
are defined up to operators
\Sigma(g)\equiv\Sigma_{a}(g)\cdot\Sigma_{b}(g)
that acts on
\partial M=\{a,b\}
.
Under the action of
\Sigma(g)
, we have
U_{M}(g)\to\widetilde{U}_{M}(g)=\Sigma(g)\cdot U_{M}(g)
.
In particular,
\Sigma_{a/b}
can carry opposite
U_{f}(1)
quantum number, where

\displaystyle[n_{f},\Sigma_{a/b}]=\mp\mu(g)\Sigma_{a/b}(g)

(49)

with
\mu(g)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Due to this ambiguity, the
U_{f}(1)
quantum number
\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})
of
\Omega_{a/b}
is defined up to a 2-coboundary:

\displaystyle\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\to\widetilde{\sigma}(g_{1},g_{2})=\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})+\mu(g_{1})+\mu(g_{2})-\mu(g_{1}g_{2})

(50)

Another ambiguity comes from the
U(1)
phase ambiguity when decomposing
\Omega
to
\Omega_{a}\cdot\Omega_{b}
.
Combined with the above
\Sigma
ambiguity, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})={}

\displaystyle\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\Sigma_{a}(g_{1})\cdot{}^{U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\Sigma_{a}(g_{2})

\displaystyle\cdot\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\Sigma^{-1}(g_{1}g_{2})\,.

(51)

\widetilde{\Omega}_{a/b}
also satisfy similar condition as Eq. (
46
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\tilde{\Omega}_{a}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})

(52)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}){}^{\tilde{U}_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\tilde{\Omega}_{a}(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\tilde{\Omega}_{a}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

The detailed derivation of the relation between
\widetilde{\omega}
and
\omega
is presented in Sec.II of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, where we conclude

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\frac{\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\beta(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})}{\beta(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})(\beta(g_{2},g_{3}))^{1-2s(g_{1})}}\cdot(-)^{\mu(g_{1})\tilde{\sigma}(g_{2},g_{3})+\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\mu(g_{3})}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}(-)^{\rho(g_{1}g_{2})+s(g_{1}g_{2})}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\mu(g_{3})}

(53)

Eq. (
50
) and Eq. (
53
) gives the coboundary conditions for the anomalous symmetry action at edge.

In summary, the classification of topological insulators with symmetry group
G_{f}
is given by the fermionic three-cohomology, which are constructed using fermionic three-cocycles
(\sigma,\omega)
that satisfy Eq. (
47
) and (
48
) modulo coboundaries whose definitions are presented in Eq. (
50
) and (
53
).

IV
Tensor equations for topological insulators

In this section, we delve into the exploration of the symmetry actions on the internal legs of FPEPS while making specific assumptions.
Building upon these assumptions, we systematically formulate an exhaustive set of tensor equations that encompass a wide range of possible scenarios.
From each distinct set of tensor equations, we extract crucial algebraic information, offering insights that align with the classification outcomes for topological insulators discussed in Section
III
.
Of paramount importance, these algebraic insights establish a profound link to the anomalous edge theories of topological insulators, which we meticulously elaborate upon in Section
V
.

IV.1
Symmetric FPEPS

In this subsection, we provide a brief introduction to the symmetric conditions on local tensors for FPEPS, with a focus on the symmetry groups discussed in Section
III.1
.
We begin with FPEPS composed of site tensors
\hat{T}_{s}
and bond tensors
\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}
, where
s
and
s^{\prime}
denote site coordinates.
The physical wavefunction is obtained by contracting the internal legs, which can be represented as

\displaystyle\lvert\Psi\rangle={\rm{fTr}}\left[\Bigg(\bigotimes_{\langle ss^{\prime}\rangle}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\Bigg)\otimes_{f}\Bigg(\bigotimes_{s}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}\hat{T}_{s}\Bigg)\right]

(54)

For simplicity, we adopt the convention that physical legs reside exclusively on site tensors, while all internal legs of site/bond tensors correspond to ket/bra spaces, pointing inward/outward.
For the purpose of illustration, we consider the FPEPS on a square lattice.
Nonetheless, it is important to note that the framework developed in this work can be readily extended to other lattice geometries without any loss of generality.

As discussed in Sec.I of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

,
\forall g\in G_{f}
, we assume that the action of
g
on physical legs can be pushed to gauge transformations on internal legs.
Since our focus is on onsite symmetries in this work, we can express the symmetric condition as a constraint on each single local tensor as

\displaystyle U_{s}(g)\otimes_{f}\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\right)K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}=\hat{T}_{s}~,

\displaystyle K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot K^{s(g)}\cdot\left(W^{-1}_{(s\alpha)}(g)\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g)\right)=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}

(55)

where
U_{s}(g)
are representation of
g
on physical leg
s
.

(s\alpha)
and
(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})
label internal legs, and

\displaystyle W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\left|i\right)_{(s\alpha)}=\sum_{j}[W_{(s\alpha)}(g)]_{ji}\left|j\right)_{(s\alpha)}~,

\displaystyle\left(i\right|_{(s\alpha)}W^{-1}_{(s\alpha)}(g)=\sum_{j}\left(j\right|_{(s\alpha)}\left[W_{(s\alpha)}^{-1}(g)\right]_{ij}

(56)

We note that
W(g)
’s may not be even parity, and in such cases, the order of
W(g)
(and
W^{-1}(g)
) must be carefully chosen, which ensures that
W_{(s\alpha)}(g)
and
W_{(s\alpha)}^{-1}(g)
can be moved together without additional fermion swapping gates.
As we will demonstrate in Section
VI
, such specific ordering gives rise to Kasteleyn orientation.

As an illustration, for a site tensor on square lattice, we have

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}=T_{abcd,p}|a)_{1}|b)_{2}|c)_{3}|d)_{4}\lvert p\rangle\,,

and the above symmetric condition is represented graphically as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

An example of bond tensors is illustrated as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}=B_{ss^{\prime},ab}\left(a\right|_{\alpha}\left(b\right|_{\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

with corresponding symmetry condition

\hskip-8.5359pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

For
U_{f}(1)
symmetry, the above symmetric condition are translated to zero fermion number of local tensors

3
3

3

In general, the physical wavefunction preserves
U_{f}(1)
symmetry if local tensors carry constant fermion number.
If the fermion numbers of local tensors are odd, such tensors have odd fermion parity.
However, in this work, we will not consider such cases.
:

\displaystyle\left(n_{f;s}+\sum_{\alpha}n_{f;(s\alpha)}\right)\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=0\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot(n_{f;(s\alpha)}+n_{f;(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})})=0\,.

(57)

As mentioned in Section
II
, we have identified a unique type of local tensor constraint on FPEPS.
This constraint corresponds to a gauge transformation on the internal legs while leaving each local tensor unchanged:

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}D_{(s\alpha)}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}=\hat{T}_{s}\,,\quad\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot D_{(s\alpha)}\otimes\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}D_{(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}

(58)

In this work, we direct our attention to the scenario where all IGG elements exhibit
even parity
.
As demonstrated in Sec.I of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, the IGG elements are intricately connected to the entanglement characteristics of the wavefunction.
Specifically, the presence of ”global” IGG generally induces low-energy gauge dynamics, resulting in long-range entanglement.
To circumvent this, we make further assumption that all IGG elements can be decomposed into products of plaquette IGG elements
Jiang and Ran 2017a
which solely act on the internal legs within a plaquette.

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322ptD_{(s\alpha)}=\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{2}}\cdot\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{1}}

(59)

where
p_{1/2}
label plaquette coordinate.
For later convenience, we also use
\lambda^{(1/2)}_{(s\alpha)}
to denotes action on different plaquette.

Constraints on local tensors from the plaquette IGG are

\displaystyle\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{(1)}\otimes\lambda_{(s\alpha+1)}^{(2)}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}{}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}_{s}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot\left[\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{(a)}\right]^{-1}\otimes\left[\lambda_{(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}^{(\bar{a})}\right]^{-1}{}

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\,,

(60)

with
a
taking value 1 or 2 and
\bar{a}=3-a
.

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

In the following, we demonstrate that the plaquette IGG elements belonging to different plaquettes, denoted as
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, can always be chosen to commute with each other.
This statement is evidently true when
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
have no shared leg.
Therefore, we focus on the scenario where
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are neighbouring plaquettes with
(s\alpha)
representing the shared leg.
The commutation relation between
\lambda^{p_{1}}
and
\lambda^{p_{2}}
on leg
(s\alpha)
equals that of
\lambda^{p_{1}}
and
\lambda^{p_{2}}
on all legs:

\displaystyle\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{1}}\cdot\lambda^{p_{2}}_{(s\alpha)}\cdot\left[\lambda^{p_{1}}_{(s\alpha)}\right]^{-1}\cdot\left[\lambda^{p_{2}}_{(s\alpha)}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{(s_{1}\alpha_{1})\in p_{1}}\lambda_{(s_{1}\alpha_{1})}^{p_{1}}\right)\cdot\left(\bigotimes_{(s_{2}\alpha_{2})\in p_{2}}\lambda^{p_{2}}_{(s_{2}\alpha_{2})}\right)\cdot

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{(s_{1}\alpha_{1})\in p_{1}}\left[\lambda^{p_{1}}_{(s_{1}\alpha_{1})}\right]^{-1}\right)\cdot\left(\bigotimes_{(s_{2}\alpha_{2})\in p_{2}}\left[\lambda^{p_{2}}_{(s_{2}\alpha_{2})}\right]^{-1}\right)

(61)

It is important to note that, by definition, the commutation between IGG elements should also result in an IGG element.
Furthermore, an IGG element that only acts on a single leg
(s\alpha)
can be simplified to
\hat{1}
by discarding internal states that are not invariant under such a single-leg IGG operation.
Thus, Eq. (
61
) can always be trivialized.

We would like to emphasize the presence of a special
U(1)
subgroup in the plaquette IGG, which corresponds to the decomposition of the action of
\hat{1}
on all internal legs.
Specifically,
\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p}=\exp[\pm\operatorname{i}\theta]
, where the
\pm
signs are chosen such that
\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{1}}=[\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{2}}]^{*}
for two neighboring plaquettes
p_{1,2}
sharing the leg
(s\alpha)
.
Due to this redundancy, the decomposition of
D_{(s\alpha)}
in Eq. (
59
) is not unique:

\displaystyle D_{(s\alpha)}=\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{2}}\cdot\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{1}}=\left(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta}\cdot\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{2}}\right)\cdot\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\theta}\cdot\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{p_{1}}\right)

(62)

In our investigation, we will also encounter nontrivial
U(1)
IGG in the subsequent analysis.
In such cases, the condition for ”global” IGG in Eq. (
58
) can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}n_{D;(s\alpha)}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}=0\,,\quad\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot\left(n_{D;(s\alpha)}+n_{D;(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}\right)=0

(63)

where
\exp[\operatorname{i}\theta n_{D}]
is an
U(1)
IGG element, and the generator
n_{D}
is named as an IGA element.
Such nontrivial
n_{D}
gives rise to
U(1)
gauge dynamics, which we aim to eliminate to study the SRE phase.
To achieve this, we assume the decomposition into plaquette IGA as follows:

\displaystyle n_{D;(s\alpha)}=n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(2)}+n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(1)}

(64)

where
\exp[\operatorname{i}\theta n_{\lambda}^{p}]
is an plaquette IGG, giving constraints on local tensors as

\displaystyle\left(n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(1)}+n_{\lambda;(s\alpha+1)}^{(2)}\right)\cdot\hat{T}^{s}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot\left(n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}+n_{\lambda;(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}^{(\bar{a})}\right)

\displaystyle=0\,,~a=1/2

(65)

And Eq. (
61
) leads to the following commutation relation

\displaystyle[n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{p_{1}},~n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{p_{2}}]=0

(66)

Similar as Eq. (
62
), the decomposition in Eq. (
64
) is also not unique:

\displaystyle n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(2)}+n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(1)}=(n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(2)}+\varepsilon)+(n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(1)}-\varepsilon)

(67)

where
\varepsilon
is a constant.

IV.2
Tensor equations for symmetric FPEPS

As in the example discussed in Section
II
, the presence of IGG leads to distinct multiplication rules for the symmetry action on internal legs compared to those on physical legs (Eq. (
32
)).
Namely, symmetries act as projective representations on internal legs, with IGG serving as the coefficient.
A well-known example is the MPS representation of AKLT states, where the physical legs are spin-1 states, while the internal legs support a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
, representing a projective representation of the
SO(3)
symmetry with the coefficient in the form of a
U(1)
phase IGG.
The characterization of such projective representation on internal legs is described by equations known as
tensor equations
.
In the following, we will discuss the tensor equations for the symmetry group
G_{f}
with multiplication rules given by Eq. (
35
) and (
36
).

IV.2.1
Commutators between
n_{f}
and elements in
G_{b}

By pushing Eq. (
34
) to internal legs, we obtain:

\displaystyle W(g)K^{s(g)}\circ U_{f}(\theta)=U_{D;g}\left((-)^{s(g)}\theta\right)\cdot U_{f}\left((-)^{\rho(g)+s(g)}\theta\right)

(68)

Here,
U_{D;g}(\theta)
is an IGG element, and can be expressed using its generator
n_{D}(g)
as
\exp[\operatorname{i}\theta n_{D}(g)]
.
In the following discussion, we will occasionally omit the internal leg index
(s\alpha)
when there is no ambiguity.
Expressed in terms of
n_{D}
’s, we find from Eq. (
35
) that Eq. (
68
) becomes

\displaystyle W(g)\circ n_{f}=(-)^{\rho(g)}n_{f}+n_{D}(g)\,,

(69)

As
U_{f}(0)=U_{f}(2\pi)=1
, we conclude eigenvalues of
n_{D}
’s are all integers.

As mentioned earlier, the
U(1)
IGG symmetry leads to long-range entanglement, which can be eliminated through the plaquette IGG decomposition given by Eq. (
59
):

\displaystyle U_{D;g}(\theta)=U_{\lambda;g}^{(2)}(\theta)\cdot U_{\lambda;g}^{(1)}(\theta)

(70)

By expressing
U_{\lambda;g}^{(a)}(\theta)
as
\exp[\operatorname{i}\theta n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)]
, we get decomposition of
n_{D}(g)
to
n_{\lambda}(g)
as

\displaystyle n_{D}(g)=n_{\lambda}^{(2)}(g)+n_{\lambda}^{(1)}(g)\,.

(71)

Here,
n_{D}(g)
’s and
n_{\lambda}^{p}
’s carry zero fermion charge.

In the following, we establish consistent conditions for
n_{D}(g)
’s and
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)
’s.
There are two ways to act
g_{1}g_{2}
on
n_{f}
on internal legs, resulting in the same outcome:

\displaystyle W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}\circ n_{f;(s\alpha)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\circ n_{f;(s\alpha)}\,,

(72)

The derivation of this equation involves intricate details, and we relegate it to Sec.III of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

for a comprehensive treatment.
We can introduce the notation
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-7.56738pt{g}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-7.56738pt{g}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-4.84721pt{g}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-4.00688pt{g}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{f}\equiv W(g)K^{s(g)}\circ n_{f}
, which is unambiguous based on the aforementioned equation.
Combining this with Eq. (
69
), we obtain:

(-)^{\rho(g_{2})}\cdot n_{D}(g_{1})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.86276pt{g_{1}}\kern 22.98871pt}}_{{\kern-22.59982pt\kern 22.98871pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.86276pt{g_{1}}\kern 22.98871pt}}_{{\kern-22.59982pt\kern 22.98871pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-19.16617pt{g_{1}}\kern 15.0101pt}}_{{\kern-14.62122pt\kern 15.0101pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-16.51898pt{g_{1}}\kern 12.36292pt}}_{{\kern-11.97403pt\kern 12.36292pt}}}=n_{D}(g_{1}g_{2})\,,

(73)

where
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-7.56738pt{g}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-7.56738pt{g}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-4.84721pt{g}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-4.00688pt{g}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{D}\equiv W(g)K^{s(g)}\circ n_{D}
.

Similarly, by acting
g_{1}g_{2}
on
U_{f}(1)
and utilizing Eq. (
68
), we acquire

\displaystyle\exp\left[\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1}g_{2})+\rho(g_{2})}\theta\cdot n_{D}(g_{1})\right]\cdot\exp\left[\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\theta\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.86276pt{g_{1}}\kern 22.98871pt}}_{{\kern-22.59982pt\kern 22.98871pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.86276pt{g_{1}}\kern 22.98871pt}}_{{\kern-22.59982pt\kern 22.98871pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-19.16617pt{g_{1}}\kern 15.0101pt}}_{{\kern-14.62122pt\kern 15.0101pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-16.51898pt{g_{1}}\kern 12.36292pt}}_{{\kern-11.97403pt\kern 12.36292pt}}}\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\exp\left[\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\theta\cdot n_{D}(g_{1}g_{2})\right]

(74)

Through a comparison between Eq. (
73
) and (
74
), it is reasonable to assume that
[n_{D}(g_{1}),\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.86276pt{g_{1}}\kern 22.98871pt}}_{{\kern-22.59982pt\kern 22.98871pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.86276pt{g_{1}}\kern 22.98871pt}}_{{\kern-22.59982pt\kern 22.98871pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-19.16617pt{g_{1}}\kern 15.0101pt}}_{{\kern-14.62122pt\kern 15.0101pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n_{D}(g_{2})^{{\kern-16.51898pt{g_{1}}\kern 12.36292pt}}_{{\kern-11.97403pt\kern 12.36292pt}}}]=0
.
After performing straightforward calculations, as detailed in Sec.III of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, we arrive at the following simple commutation relation:

[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{{}_{D,j}}(g_{2}),\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{3}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{3}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{3}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{3}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{{}_{D,j}}(g_{4})]=0,~~\forall g_{1,2,3,4}\in G_{b}\,.

(75)

Based on the above discussion on
n_{D}
’s, we come to derive relations for
n_{\lambda}
’s.
Due to the ambiguity presented in Eq. (
67
),
n_{\lambda}
’s satisfy similar relations as in Eq. (
73
) up to an integer function
\sigma:G_{b}\times G_{b}\to\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{2})}\cdot n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{1})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{2})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{1}g_{2})+(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2})}\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\,,~a=1,2

(76)

Here, we add
(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2})}
to match results in Section
III
(see details in Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

).
Similar as
n_{D}
’s, we also assume
n_{\lambda}
’s satisfy the following simple commutation relations:

[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}^{(a)}_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}(g_{2}),\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{3}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{3}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{3}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{3}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{4})]=0,~~\forall g_{1,2,3,4}\in G_{b}\,.

(77)

Here,
\sigma
’s are two-cocycles, which satisfy Eq. (
47
).
To see this, let us decompose
n_{\lambda}(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})
in two different ways using Eq. (
76
):

\displaystyle n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})={}

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{3})}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{1}g_{2})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-14.58032pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-14.58032pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-11.14217pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-10.30183pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{3})-(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})}\sigma(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})

(78)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{2}g_{3})}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{1})+(-)^{\rho(g_{3})}\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{2})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-14.58032pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-14.58032pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-11.14217pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-10.30183pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{3})-(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})}\cdot(\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})+\sigma(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3}))

We also have

\displaystyle n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})={}

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{2}g_{3})}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{1})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{2}g_{3})-(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})}\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

(79)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{2}g_{3})}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{1})+(-)^{\rho(g_{3})}\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{2})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-14.58032pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-14.58032pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-11.14217pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}g_{2}}}}}n^{{\kern-10.30183pt{g_{1}g_{2}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{3})-(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})}((-)^{\rho(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})+\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}))

Thus, by comparing the above two equations, we recover Eq. (
47
):

\displaystyle\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})+\sigma(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=(-)^{\rho(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})+\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

(80)

IV.2.2
Fusion of symmetry flux in
G_{b}

We now consider representation of Eq. (
36
) on internal legs.
According to Eq. (
55
