), when acting
(g_{1},0)\cdot(g_{2},0)
on local site tensor
\hat{T}_{s}
and bond tensor
\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{s}={}

\displaystyle\left[U_{s}(g_{1})\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\right)K^{s(g_{1})}\right]\cdot\left[U_{s}(g_{2})\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{2})}\right]\cdot\hat{T}_{s}~,

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\exp[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})n_{f;s}]\cdot U_{s}(g_{1}g_{2})\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}\exp[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})n_{f;(s\alpha)}]\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1}g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot\hat{T}^{s}

(81)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}={}

\displaystyle K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss{\prime}}\cdot K^{s(g_{2})}\cdot\left(W^{-1}_{s\alpha}(g_{2})\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}(g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{1})}\left(W_{s\alpha}^{-1}(g_{1})\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}(g_{1})\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot\left(W^{-1}_{s\alpha}(g_{1}g_{2})\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}(g_{1}g_{2})\right)\exp[-\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})(n_{f;(s\alpha)}+n_{f;(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})})]\,.

(82)

Naively, one might expect that
W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}
differs from
\exp[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})n_{f;(s\alpha)}]\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}
by an IGG element
D_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1},g_{2})
.
However, this relation is not true.
According to Eq. (
69
), the
W(g)
’s may not be parity-even, and thus, in general, one should add fermion swapping gate when move the actions of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
on the same internal legs together, and thus in general,

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\right)K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{2})}\neq\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})^{{\kern-45.2232pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 31.69221pt}}_{{\kern-31.30333pt\kern 31.69221pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})^{{\kern-45.2232pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 31.69221pt}}_{{\kern-31.30333pt\kern 31.69221pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})^{{\kern-33.226pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 20.98418pt}}_{{\kern-20.59529pt\kern 20.98418pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{2})^{{\kern-29.90248pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 17.66066pt}}_{{\kern-17.27177pt\kern 17.66066pt}}}\right)K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}

(83)

Therefore, IGG elements may not be able to capture the difference between these two ways of
g_{1}g_{2}
action.

In the following, we propose a method to circumvent fermion swapping gates when exchanging
W(g_{a})
’s acting on different internal legs.
To achieve this, we decompose
W_{(s\alpha)}(g)
based on its fermion number:

\displaystyle W_{(s\alpha)}(g)=\sum_{m}W_{m;(s\alpha)}(g)\,,\quad\text{where }\ \left[W_{m;(s\alpha)}(g),\,n_{f,j}\right]_{\rho(g)}=m\cdot W_{m;(s\alpha)}(g)\,.

(84)

Here,
[A,B]_{\rho}\equiv AB-(-)^{\rho}BA
.
Fermion parity of
W_{m;(s\alpha)}(g)
follows the even-oddness of
m
.
With such decomposition, the symmetry condition on local tensors
\hat{T}_{s}
and
\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}
reads

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{s}=U_{s}(g)\otimes\sum_{m_{0}+m_{1}+\dots=0}\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{m_{\alpha};(s\alpha)}(g)\right)K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}

(85)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}=K^{s(g)}\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}K^{s(g)}\cdot\sum_{m+m^{\prime}=0}\left(W_{m;s\alpha}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{m^{\prime};s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}(g)\right)^{-1}

(86)

Note that
\hat{T}_{s}
and
\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}
are charge neutral, which implies only terms with zero total fermion numbers remain.

We then introduce traceless matrix
\beta
with
\beta^{2}=\hat{1}
, and define

W_{\beta}(g)\equiv\sum_{m}\beta^{m}W_{m}(g)\,,

(87)

\displaystyle W_{\beta}(g)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

where zigzag legs are space for
\beta
’s.
Similarly
W^{-1}_{\beta}(g)
is defined as

\displaystyle W^{-1}_{\beta}(g)=\sum_{m}\beta^{m}W^{-1}_{m}(g)\,.

(88)

Thus, Eq. (
85
) can be expressed as

\hat{T}^{s}=d_{\beta}^{-1}\,U(g)\otimes_{f}\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{\beta;(s\alpha)}(g)\right]K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}^{s}\,,

(89)

where
d_{\beta}
is dimension for
\beta
.
The above equation can be drawn graphically as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

while for bond tensors

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}=d_{\beta}^{-1}K^{s(g)}\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}K^{s(g)}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[W^{-1}_{\beta,(s\alpha)}(g)\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{\beta,(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g)\right]\,,

which is then illustrated as:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

To describe the action of
g_{1}\cdot g_{2}
on internal legs, we introduce two traceless matrices,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
, satisfying the anti-commutation relation
{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}=0
.
This anti-commutation relation effectively cancels out the fermion sign arising from exchanging
W(g_{1/2})
’s, allowing us to move
W(g_{1/2})
together on the same internal legs:

\displaystyle\hat{T}^{s}

\displaystyle=d_{\beta}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[U_{s}(g_{1})\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{\beta_{1};(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\right)K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{s}(g_{2})\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{\beta_{2};(s\alpha)}(g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{2})}\right]\cdot\hat{T}^{s}

\displaystyle=d^{-1}_{\beta}\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[U_{s}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}U_{s}(g_{2})^{{\kern-35.87852pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 22.34753pt}}_{{\kern-21.95865pt\kern 22.34753pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}U_{s}(g_{2})^{{\kern-35.87852pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 22.34753pt}}_{{\kern-21.95865pt\kern 22.34753pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}U_{s}(g_{2})^{{\kern-26.8363pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 14.59448pt}}_{{\kern-14.2056pt\kern 14.59448pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}U_{s}(g_{2})^{{\kern-23.92093pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 11.67911pt}}_{{\kern-11.29022pt\kern 11.67911pt}}}\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{\beta_{1};(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-17.55016pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-16.0335pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}_{\beta_{2};(s\alpha)}(g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\right]\cdot\hat{T}^{s}

(90)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss{\prime}}\cdot K^{s(g_{2})}\cdot d_{\beta}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[\left(W^{-1}_{\beta_{2},(s\alpha)}(g_{2})\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{\beta_{2},(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g_{2})\right)K^{s(g_{1})}\left(W^{-1}_{\beta_{1},(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{\beta_{1},(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g_{1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss{\prime}}\cdot K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot d_{\beta}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-17.55016pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-16.0335pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}^{-1}_{\beta_{2},(s\alpha)}(g_{2})\cdot W^{-1}_{\beta_{1},(s\alpha)}(g_{1})\otimes_{f}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-17.55016pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-16.0335pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}^{-1}_{\beta_{2},(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g_{2})\cdot W^{-1}_{\beta_{1},(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g_{1})\right]

(91)

The above equation can easily be generalized to action of
g_{1}\cdot g_{2}\cdots g_{n}
: we simply consider
W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})\cdot W_{\beta_{2}}(g_{2})\cdots W_{\beta_{n}}(g_{n})
, where
\left\{\beta_{i},\beta_{j}\right\}=2\delta_{ij}
.

Comparing Eq. (
90
) and the second line in (
IV.2.2
), we conclude that for
g_{1}\cdot g_{2}
on a single internal leg
(s\alpha)

\displaystyle W_{\beta_{1};(s\alpha)}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot W_{\beta_{2};(s\alpha)}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}=D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\exp\{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot n_{f;(s\alpha)}\}\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\,,

(92)

\displaystyle~~\,\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

where
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
’s are charge neutral, which satisfy

\displaystyle d_{\beta}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[\bigotimes_{\alpha}D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}(g_{1},g_{2})\right]\cdot\hat{T}_{s}={}

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{s}

(93)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot d_{\beta}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\left[D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}(g_{1},g_{2})\right]={}

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\,.

(94)

Namely,
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}
behaves like an IGG element, but with additional
\beta
legs.

IV.2.3
Decomposing
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
to plaquette IGG

To avoid long-range entanglement, we assume that
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
’s can be further decomposed to “plaquette IGG elements” with additional
\beta
legs:

\displaystyle D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}(g_{1},g_{2})=\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})

(95)

where
\Lambda
’s satisfy

\displaystyle\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes_{f}\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha+1)}^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}_{s}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{1}}_{\beta},,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes_{f}\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}^{(\bar{a})}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{1}}_{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$};\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

where the dangling teal zigzag line represents the remaining identity matrix
\hat{\mathbf{1}}_{\beta}
in the
\beta
space.
Similar as Eq. (
62
), decomposition in Eq. (
95
) has a
U(1)
phase ambiguity:

\displaystyle D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})

(97)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta}\cdot\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\right)\cdot\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\theta}\cdot\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2});(s\alpha)}^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})\right)

Here in the main text, we show the expression for
\Lambda^{(a)}
’s without derivation, and we leave details in Sec.III of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(1)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})=\iota^{(1)}_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-7.83357pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.95952pt}}_{{\kern-2.57063pt\kern 2.95952pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-7.83357pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.95952pt}}_{{\kern-2.57063pt\kern 2.95952pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-5.89035pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.73428pt}}_{{\kern-1.3454pt\kern 1.73428pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-5.39484pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.23877pt}}_{{\kern-0.84988pt\kern 1.23877pt}}}{{}^{(1)}_{\beta_{2}}}(g_{2})\cdot\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(2)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})=\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-7.83357pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.95952pt}}_{{\kern-2.57063pt\kern 2.95952pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-7.83357pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.95952pt}}_{{\kern-2.57063pt\kern 2.95952pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-5.89035pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.73428pt}}_{{\kern-1.3454pt\kern 1.73428pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-5.39484pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.23877pt}}_{{\kern-0.84988pt\kern 1.23877pt}}}^{(2)}_{\beta_{2}}(g_{2})\cdot\iota^{(2)}_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})

(98)

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

(99)

where
\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})
belongs to plaquette IGG, namely

\displaystyle\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes_{f}\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot\lambda_{(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes_{f}\lambda_{(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}^{(\bar{a})}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}

(100)

\iota^{(a)}
’s in Eq. (
98
) are defined as

\displaystyle\iota_{\beta}^{(a)}(g)\equiv\beta^{n^{(a)}_{\lambda}(g)}\,,

(101)

which constitute a plaquette IGG, since

\displaystyle\iota_{\beta;(s\alpha)}^{(1)}\otimes\iota_{\beta;(s\alpha+1)}^{(2)}\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=\beta^{n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(1)}+n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(2)}}\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=\hat{T}^{s}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{1}}_{\beta}\,,

and similar IGG holds for bond tensor:

\displaystyle\iota_{\beta;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}\otimes\iota_{\beta;(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}^{(\bar{a})}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}=\beta^{n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}+n_{\lambda;(s^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})}^{(\bar{a})}}\cdot\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{1}}_{\beta}\,.

We also introduce
I_{\beta}(g)
for later use

\displaystyle I_{\beta}(g)=\iota_{\beta}^{(2)}(g)\cdot\iota_{\beta}^{(1)}(g)=(\beta)^{n_{D;(s\alpha)}(g)}\,,

(102)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

To proceed, we also assume the following commutation relation:

\displaystyle[\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2}),\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-30.07104pt{g_{3}}\kern 25.19699pt}}_{{\kern-24.8081pt\kern 25.19699pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-30.07104pt{g_{3}}\kern 25.19699pt}}_{{\kern-24.8081pt\kern 25.19699pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-20.74353pt{g_{3}}\kern 16.58746pt}}_{{\kern-16.19858pt\kern 16.58746pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-18.09634pt{g_{3}}\kern 13.94028pt}}_{{\kern-13.55139pt\kern 13.94028pt}}}]=0\,,\quad\forall g_{j}\in G_{b}

(103)

IV.2.4
Associativity of symmetry fusions

Equipped with expressions for
\Lambda_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
’s and
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
’s, we are now ready to derive consistent conditions from the associativity of
g_{1}\cdot g_{2}\cdot g_{3}
action on local tensors,
\forall g_{1,2,3}\in G_{b}
.
There are two different ways to fuse them on a single leg
(s\alpha)
:

\displaystyle W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot W_{\beta_{2}}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}\cdot W_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})K^{s(g_{3})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})n_{f}}\cdot W(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot W_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})K^{s(g_{3})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot I_{\beta_{2}}(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot D_{(\beta_{2},\beta_{3})}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}(\alpha(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})+\alpha(g_{1},g_{2}))n_{f}}\cdot W(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})}\,,

(104)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

where we use the following equation in the third line:

\displaystyle W_{\beta;(s\alpha)}(g)=I_{\beta;(s\alpha)}(g)\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\,,\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

(105)

Another fusion way gives

\displaystyle W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot W_{\beta_{2}}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}\cdot W_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})K^{s(g_{3})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot D_{(\beta_{2},\beta_{3})}(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n_{f}}\cdot W(g_{2}g_{3})K^{s(g_{2}g_{3})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}[D_{(\beta_{2},\beta_{3})}(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n_{f}}\cdot I_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})]^{{\kern-143.35948pt{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 111.21643pt}}_{{\kern-110.82755pt\kern 111.21643pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}[D_{(\beta_{2},\beta_{3})}(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n_{f}}\cdot I_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})]^{{\kern-143.35948pt{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 111.21643pt}}_{{\kern-110.82755pt\kern 111.21643pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}[D_{(\beta_{2},\beta_{3})}(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n_{f}}\cdot I_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})]^{{\kern-107.73393pt{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 80.20357pt}}_{{\kern-79.81468pt\kern 80.20357pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}[D_{(\beta_{2},\beta_{3})}(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n_{f}}\cdot I_{\beta_{3}}(g_{3})]^{{\kern-98.91225pt{W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 71.38188pt}}_{{\kern-70.993pt\kern 71.38188pt}}}\cdot D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{3})}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})n_{f}}\cdot W(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3})}\,,

(106)

where
\beta_{1,2,3}
anticommute with each other.

Then, by decomposing
D
’s in Eq. (
104
) and (
106
) to
\Lambda
’s by Eq. (
95
), and further to
\lambda
’s by Eq. (
98
), we are able to derive consistent conditions for
\lambda^{(1/2)}
’s:

\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=\frac{(-)^{{n_{\lambda}^{(2)}(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})}}{\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1})}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n^{(2)}_{\lambda}(g_{1})}\right)\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-28.05046pt{g_{1}}\kern 23.8944pt}}_{{\kern-23.50551pt\kern 23.8944pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-24.32005pt{g_{1}}\kern 20.16399pt}}_{{\kern-19.7751pt\kern 20.16399pt}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})\,,

(107)

as well as

\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})(-)^{{n_{\lambda}^{(1)}(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1})}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n^{(1)}_{\lambda}(g_{1})}\right)\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-28.05046pt{g_{1}}\kern 23.8944pt}}_{{\kern-23.50551pt\kern 23.8944pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-24.32005pt{g_{1}}\kern 20.16399pt}}_{{\kern-19.7751pt\kern 20.16399pt}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})\,.

(108)

The derivations are quite lengthy, and we list details in Sec.IV of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

We mention that that the use of
\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
is intentionally, as it satisfies 3-cocycle condition similar (but not the same) as Eq. (

), see details in Sec.IV of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

:

\displaystyle\frac{\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3},g_{4})[\omega^{\prime}(g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})]^{1-2s(g_{1})}}{\omega^{\prime}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}g_{4})}=(-)^{\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\sigma(g_{3},g_{4})}~\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}(-)^{\tilde{\rho}(g_{1}g_{2})}\alpha(g_{3},g_{4})\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})}

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\cdot\exp\big[-\operatorname{i}(-)^{\tilde{\rho}(g_{1})}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})+\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\sigma(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})\big]\,.

(110)

As we will see in Section

IV.3
Solutions to tensor equations of interacting TI

Let us summarize results for tensor equations describing symmetry actions on internal legs of interacting TI.
Given a fermionic 3-cocycle data
(\sigma,\omega^{\prime})
with consistent conditions in Eq. (
80
) and (
109
), we are able to list a set of tensor equations.
Firstly, due to the presence of IGG,
G_{f}
are represented projectively on internal legs as

\displaystyle W(g)K^{s(g)}\circ n_{f}

\displaystyle=(-)^{\rho(g)}n_{f}+n_{D}(g)\,,

\displaystyle W_{\beta_{1}}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot W_{\beta_{2}}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}

\displaystyle=D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\exp\{\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot n_{f}\}\cdot W(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\,,\quad\forall g_{j}\in G_{b}

(111)

where
W_{\beta_{j}}(g_{j})
is defined in Eq. (
87
) with
\{\beta_{i},\,\beta_{j}\}=2\delta_{ij}
.

Secondly, as TI phases are short-range entangled,
n_{D}(g)
and
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
, which may lead to long-range entanglement, should be decomposed as plaquette IGA/IGG elements:

\displaystyle n_{D}(g)

\displaystyle=n_{\lambda}^{(2)}(g)+n_{\lambda}^{(1)}(g)\,,

\displaystyle D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{(2)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\Lambda^{(1)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})

(112)

Note that such decomposition is unique up to
U(1)
phase, as seen from Eq. (
62
) and (
67
).
Here,
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)
is an plaquette IGA element.
And

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(a)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})=\iota_{\beta_{1}}^{(a)}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-7.83357pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.95952pt}}_{{\kern-2.57063pt\kern 2.95952pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-7.83357pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.95952pt}}_{{\kern-2.57063pt\kern 2.95952pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-5.89035pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.73428pt}}_{{\kern-1.3454pt\kern 1.73428pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\iota^{{\kern-5.39484pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.23877pt}}_{{\kern-0.84988pt\kern 1.23877pt}}}_{\beta_{2}}^{(a)}(g_{2})\cdot\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})

(113)

where
\lambda^{(a)}
belongs to plaquette IGG, and
\iota_{\beta}\equiv\beta^{n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)}
.
We further assume the following simple commutation relations:

\displaystyle[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g_{2}),\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{3}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{3}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{3}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{3}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(b)}(g_{4})]=[\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2}),\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(b)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-29.58386pt{g_{3}}\kern 24.70981pt}}_{{\kern-24.32092pt\kern 24.70981pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(b)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-29.58386pt{g_{3}}\kern 24.70981pt}}_{{\kern-24.32092pt\kern 24.70981pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(b)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-20.39555pt{g_{3}}\kern 16.23949pt}}_{{\kern-15.8506pt\kern 16.23949pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{3}}}}}n_{\lambda}^{(b)}(g_{4})^{{\kern-17.74837pt{g_{3}}\kern 13.5923pt}}_{{\kern-13.20341pt\kern 13.5923pt}}}]=0

(114)

Lastly, from fusion rules of symmetry defects (or associativity of group multiplication),
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)
’s and
\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})
satisfy the following relations:

\displaystyle(-)^{\rho(g_{2})}\cdot n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{1})+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-9.81738pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-7.09721pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-6.25688pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{2})=n_{\lambda;(s\alpha)}^{(a)}(g_{1}g_{2})+(-)^{a+\rho(g_{1}g_{2})}\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2})\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})(-)^{{n_{\lambda}^{(1)}(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1})}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n^{(1)}_{\lambda}(g_{1})}\right)\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-28.05046pt{g_{1}}\kern 23.8944pt}}_{{\kern-23.50551pt\kern 23.8944pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-24.32005pt{g_{1}}\kern 20.16399pt}}_{{\kern-19.7751pt\kern 20.16399pt}}}\lambda^{(1)}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

\displaystyle\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=\frac{(-)^{{n_{\lambda}^{(2)}(g_{1})}\sigma(g_{2},g_{3})}}{\omega^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{i}(-)^{s(g_{1})}\alpha(g_{2},g_{3})n^{(2)}_{\lambda}(g_{1})}\right)\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-40.46278pt{g_{1}}\kern 35.58873pt}}_{{\kern-35.19984pt\kern 35.58873pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-28.05046pt{g_{1}}\kern 23.8944pt}}_{{\kern-23.50551pt\kern 23.8944pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{2},g_{3})^{{\kern-24.32005pt{g_{1}}\kern 20.16399pt}}_{{\kern-19.7751pt\kern 20.16399pt}}}\lambda^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})

(115)

In the following, we focus on the case where
G_{b}
is a finite group with order
\left\lvert G_{b}\right\rvert
.
Then, for a given three cocycle data
(\sigma,\omega^{\prime})
for
G_{f}
, we list a solution for above tensor equations, where details are presented in Sec.V of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

We consider systems on bipartite lattices with sublattice indices
u,v
.
For an internal leg
(s\alpha)
, its dimension equals
\left\lvert G_{b}\right\rvert^{2}
, and its basis states are labeled as
\lvert l,r\rangle_{(s\alpha)}
, where
l,r\in G_{b}
.
As before, internal legs point from bond tensors to site tensors.

Fermion charge number operators act as

\displaystyle n_{f;(u\alpha)}\lvert l,r\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sigma(r,r^{-1}l)\lvert l,r\rangle

\displaystyle n_{f;(v\alpha)}\lvert l,r\rangle

\displaystyle=\sigma(l,l^{-1}r)\lvert l,r\rangle

(116)

where
\sigma
is the given twisted 2-cocycle satisfying Eq. (
80
).
For
g\in G_{b}
, its action on internal legs reads

\displaystyle W_{(u\alpha)}(g)\lvert l,r\rangle

\displaystyle=(-)^{\sigma(g,r)\sigma(r,r^{-1}l)}\cdot\omega^{-1}(g,r,r^{-1}l)\lvert gl,gr\rangle

\displaystyle W_{(v\alpha)}(g)\lvert l,r\rangle

\displaystyle=(-)^{\sigma(g,r)\sigma(l,l^{-1}r)}\cdot\omega(g,l,l^{-1}r)\lvert gl,gr\rangle

(117)

where
\omega
are related to
\omega^{\prime}
by Eq. (
110
).
By acting
W(g)
on
n_{f}
, one obtains
n_{D}(g)
, which can further be decomposed to
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)
.
And by studying
g_{1}\cdot g_{2}
on internal legs, one is able to obtain
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})(g_{1},g_{2})}
and further
\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})
.
In Sec.V of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, we carefully verify that
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)
’s and
\lambda^{(a)}(g_{1},g_{2})
’s are indeed solutions for Eq. (
115
).

V
Edge theories from tensor equation

In this section, we delve into the physical interpretation of the fermionic three-cocycle derived from the tensor equations.
By constructing edge theories based on FPEPS, we uncover an intriguing connection between the fusion rules of symmetry defects and the fermionic three-cocycle data.
These fusion rules play a crucial role in characterizing the quantum anomaly of edge theories for topological insulator phases.

V.1
Edge Hilbert space

Let us first identify the edge Hilbert space from an infinite FPEPS.
Given an infinite FPEPS, we cut a region
A
from it, whose boundary legs are labeled as
\partial A=\{1\cdots L\}
:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

Here, we assume that all boundary legs are bra states pointing outwards.

By contracting all internal legs within
A
, we get a linear map
\hat{T}_{A}
from boundary legs (
\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}
) to physical legs within
A
(
\mathbb{H}_{A}
), which reads

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{A}=\sum(T_{A})_{i_{b}i_{e}}\lvert i_{b}\rangle\langle i_{e}\rvert

(118)

For large enough
A
,
\dim\mathbb{H}_{A}\gg\dim\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}
, so such map can never be surjective.

One can construct a parent Hamiltonian
H
Schuch et al. 2010
, where the infinite FPEPS belongs to ground state manifold.
By restrict
H
to region
A
with some additional boundary terms, one obtains
H_{A}
.
It is then reasonable to assume that
\imag{\hat{T}_{A}}
to be the low-energy space of
H_{A}
.
For gapped phases,
\imag{\hat{T}_{A}}
is identified as edge Hilbert space of
H_{A}
, labeled as
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
.
From the isomorphism theorem, we have

\mathbb{H}_{edge}\simeq\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}/{\rm{ker}}\,\hat{T}_{A}\,.

(119)

According to definition of IGG, for
J\in\mathrm{IGG}
,
\hat{T}_{A}\cdot J_{\partial A}=\hat{T}_{A}
, where
J_{\partial A}=\otimes_{j\in\partial A}J_{j}
.
Namely,

\displaystyle\imag{(\hat{1}_{\partial A}-J_{\partial A})}\simeq\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}/\ker{(\hat{1}_{\partial A}-J_{\partial A})}\subseteq\ker\hat{T}_{A}

(120)

For large enough region
A
, we further assume that

\displaystyle\ker{\hat{T}_{A}}=\bigcup_{J\in IGG}\imag{(\hat{1}_{\partial A}-J_{\partial A})}\,,

(121)

and thus edge Hilbert space are formed by those boundary states that are invariant under action of all IGG elements:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{edge}\simeq\ker{(\hat{1}_{\partial A}-J_{\partial A})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\{\lvert\psi_{\partial A}\rangle~|~J_{\partial A}\lvert\psi_{\partial A}\rangle=\lvert\psi_{\partial A}\rangle,~\forall J\in{\rm{IGG}}\}\,.

(122)

For later convenience, we construct projector to
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
as

P_{edge}=T_{A}^{-1}\cdot T_{A}\,,

(123)

where
T_{A}^{-1}
:
\mathbb{H}_{A}\to\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}
is a pseudo-inverse of
T_{A}
.
According to Eq. (
122
),
P_{edge}
are defined by the following relation:

P_{edge}=J_{\partial_{A}}\cdot P_{edge}=P_{edge}\cdot J_{\partial A}\,,~\forall J\in\mathrm{IGG}

(124)

For FSPT phases, all IGG elements can be decomposed to product of plaquette IGG elements, and thus

P_{edge}=\prod_{j=1}^{L}P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(125)

where
j+\frac{1}{2}
is as boundary plaquette index.
As IGG belongs to different plaquettes commute, we have

\displaystyle P_{j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}}P_{j_{2}+\frac{1}{2}}=P_{j_{2}+\frac{1}{2}}P_{j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}}

(126)

With Eq. (
124
), Eq. (
71
), and Eq. (
98
), we have

\displaystyle P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(n^{(1)}_{\lambda,j+\frac{1}{2}}(g)+n^{(2)}_{\lambda,j+\frac{1}{2}}(g)\right)=0,~~\forall g\in G_{b}

(127)

\displaystyle P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\lambda^{(1)}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\right)=P_{j+\frac{1}{2}},~~\forall g_{1,2}\in G_{b}

Here,
n_{\lambda,j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(a)}
’s and
\lambda^{(a)}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}
’s act on boundary leg
(j+a-1)
.

V.2
Symmetry defects on the edge

For system defined on
A
, symmetry
g
acts on
\mathbb{H}_{A}
as
U_{A}(g)K^{s(g)}
, where
U_{A}(g)\equiv\bigotimes_{i\in A}U_{i}(A)
.
As
\hat{T}_{A}
is cut from symmetric infinite FPEPS, we have

\displaystyle U_{A}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}_{A}=\hat{T}_{A}\cdot W_{\partial A}(g)K^{s(g)}

(128)

Then, symmetry operation on
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
is defined as

\displaystyle U_{edge}(g)K^{s(g)}\equiv P_{edge}\cdot W_{\partial A}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot P_{edge}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{A}^{-1}\cdot U_{A}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}_{A}\cdot P_{edge}=\hat{T}_{A}^{-1}\cdot U_{A}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}_{A}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P_{edge}\cdot W_{\partial A}(g)K^{s(g)}

(129)

where we use Eq. (
123
) and (
128
) in the second line.
Here,

\displaystyle W_{\partial A}=\bigotimes_{j\in\partial A}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}\overline{W}_{j}(g)

(130)

where
\overline{W}_{j}
is either
W_{j}
or
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{{\kern-12.53989pt{F}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{{\kern-12.53989pt{F}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{{\kern-8.15625pt{F}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{{\kern-6.63959pt{F}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}_{j}
according to the rule of Kasteleyn orientation, see details in Section
VI
.
We will drop the “bar” notation over
W
, as it does not affect the algebra later on.

In this work,
P_{edge}
can be decomposed as in Eq. (
125
), and we have additional constraint comparing to Eq. (
129
):

\displaystyle P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot W_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{j+1}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot W_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{j+1}(g)K^{s(g)}

(131)

To see this, we consider
\lvert\psi\rangle\in\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}
, but
P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\lvert\psi\rangle=0
, and the above equation holds iff

\displaystyle P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot W_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{j+1}(g)K^{s(g)}\lvert\psi\rangle=0

(132)

By definition of
\lvert\psi\rangle
, there exists a plaquette IGG element
\lambda_{j+\frac{1}{2}}
, such that

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(1)}\otimes\lambda_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}\right)\cdot\lvert\psi\rangle=\alpha\lvert\psi\rangle\,,~\text{with }\alpha\neq 1

(133)

We define

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\equiv W_{\partial A}(g)K^{s(g)}\circ\lambda_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\left(W_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{j+1}(g)K^{s(g)}\right)\circ\lambda_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(a)}

(134)

And thus

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(1)}\otimes\tilde{\lambda}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}\right)\cdot W_{j}(g)\otimes W_{j+1}(g)K^{s(g)}\lvert\psi\rangle

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot W_{j}(g)\otimes W_{j+1}(g)K^{s(g)}\lvert\psi\rangle

(135)

which proves Eq. (
132
).

With the aforementioned definitions, we aim to extract the anomaly cocycle data of the edge theories following the procedure outlined in Section
III.2
.
As shown in Eq. (
38
) and (
39
), edge Hilbert space has a tensor product structure, and
U_{f}(1)
acts as onsite symmetry,
These two properties in general are not satisfied for
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
obtained in Eq. (
122
).
To reconcile this, we consider FPEPS with each internal leg expressed as a tensor product of three legs: two side-legs supporting spins and one middle leg supporting a spinless fermion.
Then, operators
n_{\lambda;j-\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}(g)
and
n_{\lambda;j+\frac{2}{1}}^{(1)}(g)
act on the left and right side-legs, respectively, while
n_{f;j}
acts on the middle leg.
As a result,
P_{j+\frac{1}{2}}
identifies the right side-leg of site
j
with the left side-leg of site
j+1
.
In this way, the edge Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}_{edge}
becomes a tensor product of local spins on plaquettes and local fermions on sites.

With Eq. (
125
) and (
129
), it is straightforward to see that as in Section
III.2
,
U_{edge}
is a finite depth local unitary circuits, which satisfies

\displaystyle U_{edge}(g_{a})K^{s(g_{a})}\circ n_{f,{\partial A}}=(-1)^{\rho(g_{a})}\cdot n_{f,{\partial A}}\cdot P_{edge}\,,

\displaystyle U_{edge}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{edge}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot n_{f;\partial A}\right]\cdot U_{edge}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\,,

where
n_{f;{\partial A}}\equiv\sum_{j}n_{f;j}
.

In analogy to Section
III.2
, the extraction of anomaly data relies on the fusion rule of symmetry defects.
Therefore, to extract this data, we begin by constructing symmetry defects using the FPEPS formalism.
For a subregion
M={1,\dots,l}
of
\partial A
,
g
-defects at the two ends can be created through the following operation:

\displaystyle U_{M}(g)=P_{edge}\cdot w_{l+1}(g)w_{L}(g)\cdot W_{M}(g)\cdot P_{edge}\,,

(136)

where
W_{M}(g)=\bigotimes_{f,j=1}^{l}W_{j}(g)
.

w_{L}(g)
and
w_{l+1}(g)
are local operators at two ends of
M
, which are chosen to give the following equation

\displaystyle U_{M}(g)=P_{edge}\cdot w_{l+1}(g)w_{L}(g)\cdot W_{M}(g)

With Eq. (
131
), it requires

\displaystyle P_{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(w_{L}(g)\cdot W_{1}(g))

\displaystyle=P_{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(w_{L}(g)\cdot W_{1}(g))\cdot P_{\frac{1}{2}}

(137)

\displaystyle P_{l+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(w_{l+1}(g)\cdot W_{l}(g))

\displaystyle=P_{l+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(w_{l+1}(g)\cdot W_{l}(g))\cdot P_{l+\frac{1}{2}}\,.

Furthermore,
U_{M}(g)
are defined to be charge neutral, namely,

\displaystyle 0=[U_{M}(g),n_{{}_{f,{\partial A}}}]_{\rho(g)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P_{edge}\cdot\big([w_{l+1}(g),n_{{}_{f,l+1}}]_{\rho(g)}w_{L}(g)+n_{{}_{D,M}}w_{l+1}(g)w_{L}(g)

\displaystyle+w_{l+1}(g)[w_{L}(g),n_{{}_{f,L}}]_{\rho(g)}\big)\cdot W_{M}(g)\cdot P_{edge}

(138)

which in turn put constraint on
w_{L/(l+1)}(g)
, where

\displaystyle[w_{L}(g),n_{{}_{f,L}}]_{\rho(g)}

\displaystyle=n^{(1)}_{\lambda,\frac{1}{2}}(g)\cdot w_{L}(g)\,

\displaystyle[w_{l+1}(g),n_{{}_{f,l+1}}]_{\rho(g)}

\displaystyle=n^{(2)}_{\lambda,l+\frac{1}{2}}(g)\cdot w_{l+1}(g)\,,

(139)

We also provide fermion parity of
w_{L/(l+1)}(g)
for later reference:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{L}(g)^{{\kern-24.15324pt{F}\kern 20.2106pt}}_{{\kern-19.82172pt\kern 20.2106pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{L}(g)^{{\kern-24.15324pt{F}\kern 20.2106pt}}_{{\kern-19.82172pt\kern 20.2106pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{L}(g)^{{\kern-15.69374pt{F}\kern 12.84583pt}}_{{\kern-12.45694pt\kern 12.84583pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{L}(g)^{{\kern-12.84692pt{F}\kern 9.99901pt}}_{{\kern-9.61012pt\kern 9.99901pt}}}

\displaystyle=(-)^{n^{(1)}_{\lambda,\frac{1}{2}}(g)}w_{L}(g)\,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{l+1}(g)^{{\kern-28.6381pt{F}\kern 24.69547pt}}_{{\kern-24.30658pt\kern 24.69547pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{l+1}(g)^{{\kern-28.6381pt{F}\kern 24.69547pt}}_{{\kern-24.30658pt\kern 24.69547pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{l+1}(g)^{{\kern-18.89722pt{F}\kern 16.0493pt}}_{{\kern-15.66042pt\kern 16.0493pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}w_{l+1}(g)^{{\kern-16.0504pt{F}\kern 13.20248pt}}_{{\kern-12.8136pt\kern 13.20248pt}}}

\displaystyle=(-)^{n^{(2)}_{\lambda,l+\frac{1}{2}}(g)}w_{l+1}(g)\,.

(140)

V.3
Fusion of symmetry defects

In the following, we will study fusion rules of symmetry defects using FPEPS.
These fusion rules are directly determined by the tensor equations derived in Section
IV
.
Interestingly, the fermionic three-cocycle data that characterizes the tensor equations also serves as the quantum anomaly data for the edge theories of TI phases.

Let us rewrite the symmetry fusion equation in Eq. (
42
) here:

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{M}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}

(141)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\exp\left[\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot n_{{}_{f,\overline{M}}}\right]\cdot

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1}g_{2})K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\,,

where
n_{{}_{f,\overline{M}}}=\sum_{j=L}^{l+1}n_{{}_{f,j}}
.
Namely, fusion of
g_{1}
- and
g_{2}
-defect differ from
g_{1}g_{2}
-defect up to local excitations
\Omega_{a/b}
at ends of
M
.

We try to calculate such fusion process based on the definition of
U_{M}(g)
in Eq. (
136
), which gives

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{M}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P_{edge}\cdot\left[w_{l+1}(g_{1})w_{L}(g_{1})W_{M}(g_{1})\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[w_{l+1}(g_{2})w_{L}(g_{2})W_{M}(g_{2})\right]\cdot P_{edge}

(142)

However, the transformation of the above equation into the form of Eq. (
141
) is not straightforward.
This is because
W_{j}(g)
does not possess a determined fermion parity, and thus rearranging
W_{j}(g)
tensors generally results in fermion swapping gates.

To avoid these swapping gates, we follow the strategy presented in Section
IV.2.2
, where we introduce additional anti-commuting matrices
\beta
’s to the definition of
U_{M}(g)
:

\displaystyle U_{M}(g)={}

\displaystyle d_{\beta}^{-1}P_{edge}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\Big[w_{{}_{\beta,l+1}}(g)\otimes_{f}w_{{}_{\beta,L}}(g)

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{l}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{\beta,j}(g)\Big]K^{s(g)}\cdot P_{edge}\,,

(143)

in which

\displaystyle W_{\beta,j}(g)=I_{\beta,j}(g)\cdot W_{j}(g)\,,

\displaystyle w_{{}_{\beta,L}}(g)=\iota_{\beta,\frac{1}{2}}^{(1)}(g)\cdot w_{L}(g)\,,

\displaystyle w_{{}_{\beta,l+1}}(g)=\iota_{\beta,l+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}(g)\cdot w_{l+1}(g)\,.

(144)

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

With this formalism, fusion of two symmetry defects reads

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1})K^{s(g_{1})}\cdot U_{M}(g_{2})K^{s(g_{2})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle d_{\beta}^{-1}P_{edge}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\Big[\left(w_{{}_{\beta_{1},l+1}}(g_{1})\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-19.5044pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.9734pt}}_{{\kern-5.58452pt\kern 5.9734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-19.5044pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.9734pt}}_{{\kern-5.58452pt\kern 5.9734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-15.88167pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.63985pt}}_{{\kern-3.25096pt\kern 3.63985pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-14.8417pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 2.59988pt}}_{{\kern-2.211pt\kern 2.59988pt}}}_{{}_{\beta_{2},l+1}}(g_{2})\right)\otimes_{f}

\displaystyle\left(w_{{}_{\beta_{1},L}}(g_{1})\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-19.5044pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.9734pt}}_{{\kern-5.58452pt\kern 5.9734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-19.5044pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.9734pt}}_{{\kern-5.58452pt\kern 5.9734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-15.88167pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.63985pt}}_{{\kern-3.25096pt\kern 3.63985pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-14.8417pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 2.59988pt}}_{{\kern-2.211pt\kern 2.59988pt}}}_{{}_{\beta_{2},L}}(g_{2})\right)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{l}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}\Big(W_{\beta_{1},j}(g_{1})\cdot

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-22.12823pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-17.55016pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}W^{{\kern-16.0335pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}_{\beta_{2},j}(g_{2})\Big)\Big]K^{s(g_{1}g_{2})}\cdot P_{edge}\,.

(145)

By Eq. (
92
), fusion of
W_{j}(g_{1})
and
W_{j}(g_{2})
gives additional
D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}(g_{1},g_{2})
, which can be further decomposed to plaquette IGG element
\Lambda
’s, as shown in Eq. (
95
).
With Eq. (
127
), such plaquette IGG element can be absorbed by
P_{edge}
, except for terms at two ends.
The derivation to obtain
\Omega_{a/b}
is quite lengthy, so we provide brief summary here, with detailed calculation presented in Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

,

For simplicity, let us ignore all the
\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})
’s,
K^{s(g)}
’s, and
\lambda(g_{1},g_{2})
’s, then

\displaystyle w_{{}_{\beta_{1},L}}(g_{1}){w}_{{}_{\beta_{2},L}}(g_{2})\sim{}

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(1)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})w_{L}(g_{1})w_{L}(g_{2})

\displaystyle w_{{}_{\beta_{1},l+1}}(g_{1}){w}_{{}_{\beta_{2},l+1}}(g_{2})\sim{}

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(2)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),l+\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})(-)^{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-8.17117pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.40399pt}}_{{\kern-4.12622pt\kern 4.40399pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-8.17117pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.40399pt}}_{{\kern-4.12622pt\kern 4.40399pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-5.35515pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.82304pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-4.75491pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.50058pt}}_{{\kern-1.22281pt\kern 1.50058pt}}}_{\lambda,l+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}(g_{2})\,{n}_{\lambda,l+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}(g_{1})}w_{l+1}(g_{1})w_{l+1}(g_{2})

\displaystyle W_{\beta_{1},j}(g_{1}){W}_{\beta_{2},j}(g_{2})\sim{}

\displaystyle D_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),j}(g_{1},g_{2})W(g_{1}g_{2})=\Lambda^{(2)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),j-\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\Lambda^{(1)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),j+\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})W_{j}(g_{1}g_{2})\,.

(146)

Eq. (
145
) is thus evaluated as

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1})\cdot U_{M}(g_{2})\sim P_{edge}\cdot

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{\beta}\Big[\prod_{j=1}^{l+1}\Lambda^{(1)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),j-\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\Lambda^{(2)}_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}),j-\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes_{f}

\displaystyle(w_{l+1}(g_{1})w_{l+1}(g_{2}))\otimes_{f}(w_{L}(g_{1})w_{L}(g_{2}))\bigotimes_{j=1}^{l}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{j}(g_{1}g_{2})\Big]\cdot P_{edge}\,.

(147)

\Lambda^{(1/2)}
’s form pairs of plaquette IGG, so are absorbed to
P_{edge}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle U_{M}(g_{1})\cdot U_{M}(g_{2})\sim P_{edge}\cdot(w_{l+1}(g_{1})w_{l+1}(g_{2}))\cdot(w_{L}(g_{1})w_{L}(g_{2}))\cdot(w^{-1}_{L}(g_{1}g_{2})w^{-1}_{l+1}(g_{1}g_{2}))\cdot U_{M}(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot P_{edge}\,,

(148)

which roughly gives

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\sim

\displaystyle{}P_{edge}\cdot w_{l+1}(g_{1})w_{l+1}(g_{2})w_{l+1}^{-1}(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot P_{edge}

(149)

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})\sim

\displaystyle{}P_{edge}\cdot w_{L}(g_{1})w_{L}(g_{2})w_{L}^{-1}(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot P_{edge}

(150)

A much more detailed and careful calculation is presented in Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, from which we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})={}

\displaystyle P_{edge}\cdot(-)^{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-8.17117pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.40399pt}}_{{\kern-4.12622pt\kern 4.40399pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-8.17117pt{g_{1}}\kern 4.40399pt}}_{{\kern-4.12622pt\kern 4.40399pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-5.35515pt{g_{1}}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.82304pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g_{1}}}}}n^{{\kern-4.75491pt{g_{1}}\kern 1.50058pt}}_{{\kern-1.22281pt\kern 1.50058pt}}}_{\lambda,l+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}(g_{2})\cdot{n}_{\lambda,l+\frac{1}{2}}^{(2)}(g_{1})}\cdot w_{l+1}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-19.5044pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.9734pt}}_{{\kern-5.58452pt\kern 5.9734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-19.5044pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 5.9734pt}}_{{\kern-5.58452pt\kern 5.9734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-15.88167pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 3.63985pt}}_{{\kern-3.25096pt\kern 3.63985pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w^{{\kern-14.8417pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 2.59988pt}}_{{\kern-2.211pt\kern 2.59988pt}}}_{l+1}(g_{2})\cdot w^{-1}_{l+1}(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\,n_{{}_{f,l+1}}}\cdot\lambda^{(1)}_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot P_{edge}

(151)

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})={}

\displaystyle P_{edge}\cdot w_{L}(g_{1})\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w_{L}(g_{2})^{{\kern-36.69159pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 23.1606pt}}_{{\kern-22.77171pt\kern 23.1606pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w_{L}(g_{2})^{{\kern-36.69159pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 23.1606pt}}_{{\kern-22.77171pt\kern 23.1606pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w_{L}(g_{2})^{{\kern-27.33765pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 15.09583pt}}_{{\kern-14.70694pt\kern 15.09583pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{K^{s(g_{1})}}}}}w_{L}(g_{2})^{{\kern-24.49083pt{K^{s(g_{1})}}\kern 12.24901pt}}_{{\kern-11.86012pt\kern 12.24901pt}}}\cdot w^{-1}_{L}(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\alpha(g_{1},g_{2})\,n_{{}_{f,L}}}\,\lambda^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot P_{edge}\,,

(152)

As shown in Section
III
, fermionic three-cocycle data are extracted by calculating fermion number of
\Omega_{a/b}
and by fusing three symmetry defects.
We now perform similar calculation with expressions in Eq. (
151
) and (
152
).

Fermion number calculations are presented in Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, which gives

\displaystyle[n_{f;\partial A},\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})]

\displaystyle=-\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})\,,

\displaystyle[n_{f;\partial A},\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})]

\displaystyle=\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})\Omega_{b}(g_{1},g_{2})\,,

(153)

where
\sigma
is defined in Eq. (
76
).
Thus, we identify
\sigma
in tensor equations with the fermion number of
\Omega_{a/b}
, which resembles Eq. (
44
).

Fusion of three symmetry defects can also be directly calculated using Eq. (
151
) and (
152
), where details are presented in Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.
There, we are able to relate
\omega^{\prime}
defined in Eq. (
107
) and (
108
) to
\omega
appeared in Eq. (
46
), which characterizes fusion anomaly of symmetry defects.

As we will show in Section
VII.2
, for the case where
G_{f}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times U_{f}(1)
and
G_{b}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{e,g\}
, fusion two
g-
symmetry flux gives additional single fermions at two ends.

VI
Gauge transformation and Kasteleyn orientation

In accordance with our approach outlined in Section
IV
and detailed in Sec.I of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, we incorporate symmetries on the physical wavefunction by imposing constraints on the local tensors.
Specifically, the symmetry on the inward/outward leg
(s\alpha)
is implemented through the action of
W_{(s\alpha)}(g)
/
W^{-1}_{(s\alpha)}(g)
, which results in the identity when contracting the leg
(s\alpha)
.
In our investigation, we encountered a significant aspect worth noting: when the transformation
W(g)
is not fermion parity even, meaning
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-22.94237pt{F}\kern 18.99973pt}}_{{\kern-18.61084pt\kern 18.99973pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-22.94237pt{F}\kern 18.99973pt}}_{{\kern-18.61084pt\kern 18.99973pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-14.48029pt{F}\kern 11.63237pt}}_{{\kern-11.24348pt\kern 11.63237pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-11.15677pt{F}\kern 8.30885pt}}_{{\kern-7.91997pt\kern 8.30885pt}}}\neq W(g)
, moving
W_{(s\alpha)}(g)
and
W^{-1}_{(s\alpha)}
together generally leads to fermion swapping gates, which disrupts the cancellation between
W
and
W^{-1}
when contracting internal legs.

To overcome this challenge, we devised a solution by extracting an oriented graph based on the order of the action of
W(g)/W^{-1}(g)
.
We demonstrated that when this graph results in a
Kasteleyn orientation

Cimasoni and Reshetikhin 2007
;
Ware et al. 2016
;
Tarantino and Fidkowski 2016
;
Cimasoni and Reshetikhin 2007
;
Ellison and Fidkowski 2019
, the FPEPS wavefunction exhibits symmetry.
In this section, we first illustrate the concept of Kasteleyn orientation using a 1D example, and then extend it to higher-dimensional cases.

VI.1
Warm up: a 1D example

We start from an FMPS wavefunction

\lvert\psi\rangle={\rm{fTr}}\left[\mathbb{B}\otimes_{f}\mathbb{T}\right]\,,

(154)

where site tensors
\mathbb{T}\equiv\otimes_{f,j}\hat{T}_{j}
, and bond tensors
\mathbb{B}\equiv\otimes_{f,j}\hat{B}_{j,j+1}
.
Each site tensors have one physical leg
j
and two incoming internal legs
(2j-1)
and
(2j)
.
We consider systems with size
L
on closed boundary, where leg
(1)
and
(2L+1)
are identified.
Note that all local tensors are parity even:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{j}

\displaystyle=F_{j}\otimes_{f}F_{(2j-1)}\otimes_{f}F_{(2j)}\cdot\hat{T}_{j}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{j,j+1}

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{j,j+1}\cdot F_{(2j+1)}\otimes_{f}F_{(2j+1)}\,.

(155)

We assume that
\lvert\psi\rangle
hosts onsite unitary symmetry
g
:

\displaystyle\lvert\psi\rangle=g\circ\lvert\psi\rangle\equiv\bigotimes_{j}U_{j}(g)\cdot\lvert\psi\rangle

(156)

which acts as gauge transformation
W(g)
on internal legs.

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{j}

\displaystyle=U_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j-1)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j)}(g)\cdot\hat{T}_{j}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{j,j+1}

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{j,j+1}\cdot W^{-1}_{(2j+1)}(g)\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{(2j)}(g)\,,

(157)

While
U(g)
’s are parity even,
W(g)
may be parity odd, which happens for certain FSPT phases:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-22.94237pt{F}\kern 18.99973pt}}_{{\kern-18.61084pt\kern 18.99973pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-22.94237pt{F}\kern 18.99973pt}}_{{\kern-18.61084pt\kern 18.99973pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-14.48029pt{F}\kern 11.63237pt}}_{{\kern-11.24348pt\kern 11.63237pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W(g)^{{\kern-11.15677pt{F}\kern 8.30885pt}}_{{\kern-7.91997pt\kern 8.30885pt}}}=-W(g)

(158)

Note that by comparing Eq. (
155
) and Eq. (
157
),
W(g)
’s on different legs must share the same parity quantum number.

Naively, Eq. (
157
) implies symmetric physical wavefunction, as
W(g)
and
W^{-1}(g)
cancels when contracting internal legs.
However, due to the odd parity of
W(g)
, moving
W(g)
and
W^{-1}(g)
together may lead to extra
-
sign from swapping fermions, and thus Eq. (
156
) holds up to
\pm
sign.
Note that in such case, the following different order of
W(g)
’s action on local tensors leads to different symmetric condition:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{j}

\displaystyle=U_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j-1)}(g)\cdot\hat{T}_{j}

\displaystyle=U_{j}(g)\otimes_{f}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W_{(2j-1)}(g)^{{\kern-36.08513pt{F}\kern 32.14249pt}}_{{\kern-31.7536pt\kern 32.14249pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W_{(2j-1)}(g)^{{\kern-36.08513pt{F}\kern 32.14249pt}}_{{\kern-31.7536pt\kern 32.14249pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W_{(2j-1)}(g)^{{\kern-24.01083pt{F}\kern 21.16292pt}}_{{\kern-20.77403pt\kern 21.16292pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W_{(2j-1)}(g)^{{\kern-20.68732pt{F}\kern 17.8394pt}}_{{\kern-17.45052pt\kern 17.8394pt}}}\otimes_{f}W_{(2j)}(g)\cdot\hat{T}_{j}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{j,j+1}

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{j,j+1}\cdot W^{-1}_{(2j)}(g)\otimes_{f}W^{-1}_{(2j+1)}(g)

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{j,j+1}\cdot W^{-1}_{(2j+1)}(g)\otimes_{f}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{-1}_{(2j)}(g)^{{\kern-32.0018pt{F}\kern 28.05916pt}}_{{\kern-27.67027pt\kern 28.05916pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{-1}_{(2j)}(g)^{{\kern-32.0018pt{F}\kern 28.05916pt}}_{{\kern-27.67027pt\kern 28.05916pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{-1}_{(2j)}(g)^{{\kern-21.09418pt{F}\kern 18.24626pt}}_{{\kern-17.85738pt\kern 18.24626pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{F}}}}W^{-1}_{(2j)}(g)^{{\kern-17.77066pt{F}\kern 14.92274pt}}_{{\kern-14.53386pt\kern 14.92274pt}}}\,,

(159)

Figure 6:
An example of 1D Kasteleyn orientation with
L=3
.
Site tensors
\hat{T}_{j}
are shaded grey, and physical indices are ignored.
Bond tensors are drawn as cyan eclipses.
Node numbers
k
from 1 to 6 are labelling the indices of
W_{(k)}(g)
’s.
Symmetry constraints on local tensors can be read from direction of arrows in the figure.

In the following, given an arbitrary choice of symmetry conditions on local tensors, we work out the
\pm
sign.
A representative choice is shown in Fig. (
6
).
Symmetry constraints are encoded in arrow orientations in the figure.
For example, if the blue arrow on site tensor
\hat{T}_{j}
points from leg
(2j-1)
to
(2j)
,
\hat{T}_{j}
satisfies Eq. (
157
).
As for bond tensor
B_{j,j+1}
, if the magenta arrow points from leg
(2j+1)
to
(2j)
, it satisfies bond tensor constraints in Eq. (
159
).

Given a specific orientation for a 1D system with size
L
and closed boundary, as shown in Fig. (
6
), such orientation can be represented by function
s
, where
s(k,k+1)=0/1
if the arrow points from
(k)/(k+1)
to
(k+1)/(k)
.
Symmetry constraint on local tensors then reads

\displaystyle U_{j}(g)\cdot\hat{T}_{j}=(-1)^{s(2j-1,2j)}\cdot\left(W_{(2j-1)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j)}(g)\right)^{-1}\cdot\hat{T}_{j}\,,\quad\hat{B}_{j,j+1}=(-1)^{s(2j,2j+1)}\cdot\hat{B}_{j,j+1}\cdot W_{(2j)}\otimes_{f}W_{(2j+1)}

(160)

We now calculate
g\circ\lvert\psi\rangle
with Eq. (
160
):

\displaystyle g\circ\lvert\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{k}s(k,k+1)}\cdot{\rm{fTr}}\left[\mathbb{B}\cdot\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{L}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{(2j)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j+1)}(g)\right)\otimes_{f}\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{L}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{(2j-1)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j)}(g)\right)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{T}\right]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left[\sum_{k}s(k,k+1)\right]+1}\cdot{\rm{fTr}}[\mathbb{B}\otimes_{f}\mathbb{T}]=(-1)^{\left[\sum_{k}s(k,k+1)\right]+1}\cdot\lvert\psi\rangle

(161)

Based on the above equation, we now introduce
Kasteleyn orientation
.
In any dimension, an oriented graph satisfies Kasteleyn orientation if, for any enclosed loop within the graph, the count of counter-clockwise oriented edges encircling it is
odd
.
For the 1D scenario, with just a single loop in consideration, the Kasteleyn orientation condition is equivalent to
\sum_{k}s(k,k+1)=1\pmod{2}
.
It has been formally established that Kasteleyn orientation exists for any planar graph possessing an even number of vertices
Cimasoni and Reshetikhin 2007
.
However, the selection of Kasteleyn orientation is not unique: by reversing the arrows on edges connected to a specific vertex
v
, one can derive an alternative orientation.
This choice extends to every vertex, allowing the option to flip or not flip arrows on edges connecting to that vertex, generating a total of
2^{N_{v}}
potential Kasteleyn orientations, with
N_{v}
representing the total vertex count.

In the context of our 1D example, if the order of
W(g)
’s action conforms to the Kasteleyn orientation, the condition expressed in Eq. (
156
) is fulfilled.
However, if this order fails to align with Kasteleyn orientation, an additional
-
sign is introduced.

Before discussing higher dimension, let us first turn our attention to a FMPS with open boundary conditions.
To facilitate our subsequent analysis, we introduce an additional bond tensor
\hat{B}_{0,1}
with outgoing internal legs
(0)
and
(1)
.
Upon contracting all internal legs within the bulk, we arrive at a large tensor
\hat{T}_{A}
, which resides on the bulk physical legs
{1,\dots,L}
and the boundary legs
(0)
and
(2L+1)
.
Symmetry transformations applied to
\hat{T}_{A}
induce gauge transformations on the boundary legs, yielding:

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{L}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}U_{j}(g)\cdot\hat{T}_{A}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{k=0}^{2L}s(k,k+1)}\cdot{\rm{fTr}}\left[\mathbb{B}\cdot\left(\bigotimes_{j=0}^{L}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{(2j)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j+1)}(g)\right)\otimes_{f}\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{L}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{(2j-1)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2j)}(g)\right)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{T}\right]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{k}s(k,k+1)}\cdot\hat{T}_{A}\cdot W_{(0)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(2L+1)}(g)

(162)

The sequence of
g
-action on the boundary legs is determined using the following method.
An additional virtual oriented line is introduced, connecting leg
(2L+1)
to
(0)
, with its orientation chosen in such a way that this closed chain adheres to Kasteleyn orientation.
The order of
W_{(0)}(g)
and
W_{(2L+1)}(g)
is then aligned with the direction of this newly introduced virtual line.
This rule is visually depicted in the figure below, where the violet dashed line indicates the order of the symmetry action on the boundary.

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$},

(163)

VI.2
Kasteleyn orientations on higher dimensions

In this subsection, we move forward to Kasteleyn orientations in higher dimensions.
For a directed graph with Kasteleyn orientations, any loop in such graph has odd number of counter-clockwise edges.
For topological insulators, symmetry transformation on internal legs may not have fixed parity, leading to fermion swapping gates when internal legs are contracted.
In the following, we define rules to extract an orientation from orders of symmetry actions on internal legs.
We claim that if such orientation is Kasteleyn, swapping gates are avoided when contracting tensors, leading to a symmetric wavefunction.
Details of the prove is left to Sec.VII of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

For simplicity, we consider FPEPS defined on a honeycomb lattice.
For
g\in G_{b}
, it acts as
W_{(s\alpha)}(g)
on internal leg
(s\alpha)
.
We focus on the case where
W_{(s\alpha)}(g)
do not have fixed parity, namely,
n_{D}(g)
in Eq. (
69
) is not a
c
-number.
We now define rules to extract orientations from action of
W(g)
’s on local tensors.
We first expand all sites of the honeycomb lattice to triangles as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\rightarrow\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}

(164)

Vertices in the new lattice live on internal legs of the honeycomb FPEPS, and are labeled as
(s\alpha)
.
Arrows on blue triangles in Eq. (
164
) are extracted from
g
-action on site tensors, while arrows on magenta lines of the original hexagons from
g
-action on bond tensors.
More specifically, from
g
-symmetric condition on tensor at site
s

\displaystyle U_{s}(g)K^{s(g)}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left[W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(s\beta)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(s\gamma)}(g)\right]^{-1}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}\,,

(165)

the arrows on the corresponding blue triangle is
(s\alpha)\rightarrow(s\beta)
,
(s\beta)\rightarrow(s\gamma)
, and
(s\alpha)\rightarrow(s\gamma)
.
For example,

\displaystyle U_{s}(g)\cdot\hat{T}^{*}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left[W_{(sx)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(sy)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(sz)}(g)\right]^{-1}\cdot\hat{T}_{s}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\scalebox{2}[2]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}}\,.

(166)

Similarly, if
g
-action on bond tensor
\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}
reads

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot K^{s(g)}=\hat{B}_{ss^{\prime}}\cdot W_{(s\alpha)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(s^{\prime}\alpha)}(g)\,,

(167)

the arrow points from
(s\alpha)
to
(s^{\prime}\alpha)
.
Following this rule,
g
-symmetry constraint on all local tensors leads to the oriented graph as in Eq. (
164
).
In Sec.VII of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, we show that the physical wavefunction is
g
-symmetric only when the right hand side of Eq. (
164
) is a Kasteleyn orientation.

VI.3
Symmetry on the open boundary from Kasteleyn orientation

Figure 7:
A Kasteleyn oriented honeycomb lattice with open boundary.
Dark green shading indicates locations of
P_{o}(g)
’s, and gray rectangles and cyan eclipses denote action of
W_{o}(g)
’s, which are parity odd.
Parity even operators
W_{e}(g)
’s act at nodes without shading.
Arrows on violet dashed lines around edge follow rules of Kasteleyn orientations: every loop, including these violet dashed lines, contains odd number of counter-clockwise orientations.

We now consider a finite 2D FPEPS on region
A
, which is cut from an infinite FPEPS, as shown in Fig.
7
.
By contracting all internal legs within
A
, we obtain a large tensor
\hat{T}_{A}
, which is a linear map from boundary virtual legs to bulk physical legs:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{A}={\rm{fTr}}_{A}[\mathbb{B}\otimes_{f}\mathbb{T}]\,,

(168)

Here, boundary legs, labeled as
j\in\left\{1,2,\dots,L\right\}
, are ordered clockwise, as shown in Fig. (
7
).

As shown in Eq. (
128
), one expects that physical symmetry action
U_{A}(g)K^{s(g)}
on
\hat{T}_{A}
can be pushed to
W_{\partial A}(g)
action on boundary legs.
To determine
W_{\partial A}(g)
for the case where
W(g)
’s do not have fixed parity, we first connect neighbouring boundary legs with dashed violet lines in Fig.
7
, whose direction are chosen such that the whole graph gives Kasteleyn orientation.

W_{\partial A}
then can be expressed as

\displaystyle W_{\partial A}(g)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{L}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}\overline{W}_{j}(g)\,,

(169)

\displaystyle\text{with }\overline{W}_{j}(g)

\displaystyle\equiv F_{j}^{\xi(j)}\circ{W_{j}(g)}=[D_{j}(g)]^{\xi(j)}\cdot{W}_{j}(g)\,.

where
\xi(j)
counts the number of dashed violet lines with counter-clockwise arrows from leg
1
to
j
.
For example, for the system in Fig.
7
,
\xi(j)
are listed as Table
1
.

\xi(1)=0

\xi(2)=1

\xi(3)=2

\xi(4)=2

\xi(5)=2

\xi(6)=3

\xi(7)=3

\xi(8)=4

\xi(9)=5

\xi(10)=6

.

Table 1:
The value of
\xi(j)
for the system in Fig.
7

Details of proof for the above statement are left in Sec.VII of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

VII
Examples for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}\times U_{f}(1)
FSPT case

In this section, we reinvestigate the TI phase presented in Section
II
, where
G_{f}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{g}\times U_{f}(1)
, and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{g}=\{e,g\}
.
We will show that this TI phase are characterized by non-trivial fermionic three cocycle data, and thus indeed fit in our general scheme of interacting topological insulators.
By solving symmetry constraints on local tensors, we obtain variational tensor wavefunctions for this phase for spin-1/2 fermionic systems on a square lattice.
Furthermore, based on symmetry transformation of internal legs (Eq. (
11
) and (
20
)) and methods developed in Section
V
, we construct edge theories for this phase, which shows quantum anomaly behaviour.

VII.1
Local Tensor Solution

Here, we consider spin-
\frac{1}{2}
fermions on square lattice, with local basis
\{\lvert 0\rangle,~c_{\uparrow}^{\dagger}\lvert 0\rangle,~c_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\lvert 0\rangle,~c_{\uparrow}^{\dagger}c_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\lvert 0\rangle\}
.
We use the bipartite feature of square lattice, where one unit cell contains two sites with sublattice indices
u
and
v
.
As we consider charge neutral system in this work, we put holes on
u
and electrons on
v
.
Namely,

\displaystyle[n_{f,u},~c_{u\sigma}^{\dagger}]=c_{u\sigma}^{\dagger}~,\quad[n_{f,v},~c_{v\sigma}^{\dagger}]=-c_{v\sigma}^{\dagger}~.

(170)

g
acts as
\sigma^{x}
on spins:

\displaystyle U(g)\cdot c_{\uparrow}\cdot U(g)=c_{\downarrow}

(171)

With translational symmetry in this bipartite lattice, there are two kinds of site tensors
\hat{T}^{u}
and
\hat{T}^{v}
, and four types of bond tensors
\hat{B}^{a}
with
a\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.
Graphically, a site tensor reads

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}=T^{s}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta,p}\left|\alpha\right)_{1}|\beta)_{2}|\gamma)_{3}|\delta)_{4}\lvert p\rangle=\hat{T}^{s}\,,

where we put physical leg at the last position.
Bond tensors are represented as

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{a}=B_{\alpha\beta}^{a}\left(\alpha\right|_{a}\left(\beta\right|_{a+2}={}

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,,

where in
B_{\alpha\beta}
the first index and second index are the legs that connect two adjacent
u-
and
v-
site respectively.
a+2
takes value module 4.
An internal leg is set to be a four dimensional Hilbert space, where basis states are labeled as
\{|ee),~|eg),~|ge),~|gg)\}
.
An internal leg can then be written as tensor product of two two-dimensional Hilbert space, and thus site tensors and bond tensors can be expressed as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}~;\quad\hskip 13.6572pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

We now give a nontrivial fermionic three cocycle
(\sigma,\omega)
, where
\sigma(g,g)=1
and
\omega(g,g,g)=\operatorname{i}
.
Other
\sigma
’s are set to be
0
, and
\omega
’s are set to be
1
.
Equipped with such cocycle, solutions for
n_{f}
,
W(g)
,
n_{\lambda}
can be read from Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.
From Eq. (S64) and Eq. (S80) in the SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, fermion charge number
n_{f}
’s are

\displaystyle n_{f,(ua)}=-\left|eg\right)\left(eg\right|~,\quad n_{f,(va)}=\left|ge\right)\left(ge\right|~.

(172)

According to Eq. (S65) and Eq. (S81) in the SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

,
W(g)
’s are expressed as

\displaystyle W_{(ua)}(g)

\displaystyle=\left|gg\right)\left(ee\right|+\operatorname{i}\left|ge\right)\left(eg\right|+\left|eg\right)\left(ge\right|+\left|ee\right)\left(gg\right|

\displaystyle W_{(va)}(g)

\displaystyle=\left|gg\right)\left(ee\right|+\left|ge\right)\left(eg\right|+\operatorname{i}\left|eg\right)\left(ge\right|+\left|ee\right)\left(gg\right|

(173)

The plaquette IGG’s (IGA’s) are also given by two-cocycle data as
From Eq. (S67) and Eq. (S83) in the SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, we obtain

\displaystyle n_{\lambda,(sa)}^{(1)}(g)

\displaystyle=-\left|ee\right)\left(ee\right|-\left|ge\right)\left(ge\right|~,

\displaystyle n_{\lambda,(sa)}^{(2)}(g)

\displaystyle=\left|ee\right)\left(ee\right|+\left|eg\right)\left(eg\right|~\,,

(174)

which is independent to
(sa)
.
The relation between
n_{\lambda}^{(a)}(g)
’s in Eq. (
76
) can be easily verified, where

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-7.56738pt{g}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-7.56738pt{g}\kern 4.94333pt}}_{{\kern-4.55444pt\kern 4.94333pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-4.84721pt{g}\kern 2.94115pt}}_{{\kern-2.55226pt\kern 2.94115pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}n^{{\kern-4.00688pt{g}\kern 2.10081pt}}_{{\kern-1.71193pt\kern 2.10081pt}}}_{\lambda}^{(1/2)}(g)=-{n}_{\lambda}^{(1/2)}(g)\mp 1\,.

(175)

n_{{}_{D}}(g)=n_{\lambda}^{(1)}(g)+n_{\lambda}^{(2)}(g)
, so

\displaystyle(-)^{n_{{}_{D}}(g)}=\left|ee\right)\left(ee\right|-\left|eg\right)\left(eg\right|-\left|ge\right)\left(ge\right|+\left|gg\right)\left(gg\right|\,.

(176)

With this expression, we can decompose
W(g)
to its fermion parity even (
E(g)
) and odd (
O(g)
) part:

\displaystyle E_{(sa)}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(-)^{n_{{}_{D}}}}{2}W_{(sa)}(g)\,;

\displaystyle O_{(sa)}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(-)^{n_{{}_{D}}}}{2}W_{(sa)}(g)\,.

We now impose symmetry and IGG constraint on site and bond tensors.
The charge neutral condition for site tensors is

\displaystyle\left(n_{f,s}+\sum_{a=1}^{4}n_{f,(sa)}\right)\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=0

(177)

For
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{Ising}
symmetry, we have

\displaystyle U_{s}(g)\cdot\bigotimes_{a=1}^{4}\mathop{{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}}W_{(sa)}(g)\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=\hat{T}^{s}.

(178)

Note that the above solution cannot be directly implemented, as entries of tensors contain fermion mode.
To take care of the fermion swapping signs, Jordan-Wigner transformation is implemented, where the Jordan-Wigner string
J
is

\displaystyle J_{(sa)}=\exp\left\{\operatorname{i}\pi n_{f,(sa)}\right\}\,,

(179)

The “bosonized”
W(g)
’s are

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-10.81126pt{b}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-10.81126pt{b}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-6.92152pt{b}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-5.40486pt{b}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}_{(sa)}(g)=E_{(sa)}(g)+\bigotimes_{j=1}^{a-1}J_{(sj)}\otimes O_{(sa)}(g)\,.

(180)

Now we can define the symmetry operations

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{Ising}^{s}={}

\displaystyle U(g)\cdot\prod_{a=1}^{4}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-10.81126pt{b}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-10.81126pt{b}\kern 8.59724pt}}_{{\kern-8.20836pt\kern 8.59724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-6.92152pt{b}\kern 5.30833pt}}_{{\kern-4.91945pt\kern 5.30833pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}}W^{{\kern-5.40486pt{b}\kern 3.79167pt}}_{{\kern-3.40279pt\kern 3.79167pt}}}_{(sa)}(g)

(181)

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}^{s}={}

\displaystyle n_{f,s}+\sum_{a=1}^{4}n_{f,(sa)}\,,

(182)

and
\hat{T}
should satisfies
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
U_{f}(1)
symmetry conditions:

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{Ising}^{s}\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=\hat{T}^{s};~\mathbb{N}^{s}\cdot\hat{T}^{s}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(183)

Furthermore, plaquette
\mathrm{IGA}
impose more constraints on
\hat{T}

\left(n^{(1)}_{\lambda,(sa)}(g)+n^{(2)}_{\lambda,(sa+1)}(g)\right)\cdot\hat{T}^{s}=0\,.

(184)

Here, plaquette
\mathrm{IGG}
simply identifies Ising spins within one plaquette, which is represented graphically as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

The bond tensor can be treated in the similar way, where the plaquette IGG gives

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\left(n_{\lambda,(ua)}^{(1)}+n_{\lambda,(va+2)}^{(2)}\right)\cdot\hat{B}^{a}=\left(n_{\lambda,(ua)}^{(2)}+n_{\lambda,(va+2)}^{(1)}\right)\cdot\hat{B}^{a}=0\,,

(185)

which identifies the legs belong to the same plaquette:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\,.

Symmetry constraints on bond tensors give

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{a}\cdot W_{(ua)}(g)\otimes_{f}W_{(va+2)}(g)={}

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{a}

(186)

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{a}\cdot(n_{f,(ua)}+n_{f,(va+2)})={}

\displaystyle 0\,.

(187)

The dimension of site tensor Hilbert space is 1024, and we have 14 linear independent solutions that satisfy Eq. (
183
) and (
184
) for
\hat{T}^{u}
(magenta color indicates the presence of fermion on the internal leg):

A valid local tensor can then be expressed as

\hat{T}^{u}=\sum_{j=1}^{14}c_{j}^{u}T_{j}\,,

(188)

with
c_{j}
being an arbitrary complex number.
The basis for site tensor
\hat{T}^{v}
is obtained by exchanging
e\leftrightarrow g
in the basis above.
The solution for bond tensor
B_{uv}
that satisfies Eq. (
185
), (
186
), and (
187
) is given as

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{a}={}

\displaystyle\left(gg\right|_{a}\left(gg\right|_{a+2}+\left(ee\right|_{a}\left(ee\right|_{a+2}

\displaystyle+\left(eg\right|_{a}\left(ge\right|_{a+2}+\left(ge\right|_{a}\left(eg\right|_{a+2}

(189)

where we use maximal entangled bond states here.

In the context of other target symmetry groups, the methodology for solving the tensor equation remains consistent with the approach described above.
This procedure involves feeding the machinery with a particular set of representations of the (twisted) group cohomology data under consideration.
Subsequently, symmetry and IGG on internal legs, such as
W(g)
,
n_{\lambda}(g)
, and
n_{f}
possess feasible solutions for any given cohomology data.
More comprehensive details regarding these solutions are elaborated in Sec.VI of SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

.

VII.2
Edge theory

In this subsection, we delve into the derivation of the edge theory for the considered topological phase, following the approach outlined in Section
V
.
To initiate this analysis, we isolate a region
A
from an infinite FPEPS structure.
Subsequently, we perform the contraction of all internal legs confined within
A
, resulting in the formation of a large tensor
\hat{T}_{A}
.
This tensor serves as a mapping from the boundary’s virtual legs
\mathbb{H}_{\partial A}
to the bulk’s physical legs
\mathbb{H}_{A}
, where the legs of
\partial A
are labeled by
{1,\dots,L}
.
Specifically, we designate a subregion
M=\{1,\dots,l\}
of
\partial A
that encompasses the first
l
sites. The associated Hilbert space is graphically represented as follows:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,

Here, spins within the same plaquette are identified by
P_{edge}
:
|r)_{n}=|l)_{n+1}

We now create symmetry defects at two ends of
M
by

\displaystyle U_{M}(g)=P_{edge}\cdot w_{l+1}(g)w_{L}(g)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{l}\mathop{}_{\mkern-4.0muf}W_{j}(g)\cdot P_{edge}\,.

U_{M}(g)
are required to be charge neutral, which impose constraints on
w_{L}(g)
and
w_{l+1}(g)
as

\displaystyle[w_{L},n_{f}]={}

\displaystyle n_{\lambda,L}^{(1)}\cdot w_{L}\,;

\displaystyle[w_{l+1},n_{f}]={}

\displaystyle n_{\lambda,l+1}^{(2)}\cdot w_{l+1}\,.

(190)

(see also Eq. (
139
)).
Following general procedure presented in Section
V.2
, we can set

\displaystyle w_{L}(g)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{l}c_{L}|le)_{L}(lg|_{L}+|lg)_{L}(le|_{L}\,;

(191)

\displaystyle w_{l+1}(g)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{r}c^{\dagger}_{l+1}|er)_{l+1}(gr|_{l+1}+|gr)_{l+1}(er|_{l+1}\,,

where
L
and
l+1
are chosen as
v-
sites.

From group relation
g^{2}=e
, one expects that by fusing two
g
-defects, one at most gets local excitations
\Omega_{a/b}
, where
a,b
labels ends of
M
:

U_{M}(g)\cdot U_{M}(g)=P_{edge}\cdot\Omega_{a}(g,g)\cdot\Omega_{b}(g,g)\cdot P_{edge}\,.

(192)

From Eq. (
152
),
\Omega_{b}
simplifies to:

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}(g,g)={}

\displaystyle P_{edge}\cdot w_{L}(g)\cdot w_{L}(g)\lambda^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}(g,g)\cdot P_{edge}\,.

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle c_{L}~\lambda^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}(g,g)\,,

(193)

which contains fermion annihilation operator, and thus gives
\sigma(g,g)=1
.
From Eq. (
46
), the three-cocycle data
\omega(g,g,g)
can be extracted by

\displaystyle\omega(g,g,g)\cdot\Omega_{b}(g,g)=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-20.3004pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-20.3004pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-15.21556pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-14.20444pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}}_{b}(g,g)\,.

(194)

According to Eq. (S68) in the SM
10
10
footnotemark:

10

, a consistent choice of
\lambda^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}(g,g)
reads
\lambda^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}(g,g)=\exp\left[\operatorname{i}\frac{\pi}{2}n^{(2)}_{\lambda,1}(g)\right]
.
Inserting this expression to Eq. (
194
), one obtains

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-20.3004pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-20.3004pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-15.21556pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{U_{M}(g)}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-14.20444pt{U_{M}(g)}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}}_{b}(g)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle c_{L}\left((-)^{n_{\lambda,1}^{(2)}(g)}\right)\left(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.36713pt{g}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-4.35419pt\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.36713pt{g}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-4.35419pt\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.7644pt{g}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-2.46945pt\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{g}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-3.94774pt{g}\kern 2.04167pt}}_{{\kern-1.65279pt\kern 2.04167pt}}}^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}(g,g)\right)=\operatorname{i}\Omega_{b}(g)

(195)
